O_{l0}=\int_{-\infty}^{u}K_{l0}(\Omega(u^{\prime}))\text{d}u^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\Omega(u)}\frac{K_{l0}(\Omega)}{F^{(0)}(\Omega)}d\Omega+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0}).

(59)

The implication of this is that, while the GW flux is
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
, it generates an
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
quasistationary contribution to the waveform. Concretely, the memory terms in equation (
56
) are

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{m}em}_{+}-ih^{\mathrm{m}em}_{\times}=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{l=2}^{\infty}\left[\epsilon h^{(1)}_{l0}(\Omega)+\epsilon^{2}h^{(2)}_{l0}(\Omega)\right]{}_{-2}Y_{l0}(\theta,\phi).

(60)

We emphasize that traditional GSF waveform models have always omitted these terms, making the models technically incomplete even at leading order in
\epsilon
.

As alluded to below equation (
18
), actually evaluating the time integral in equation (
10
) at 1PA order currently requires us to adopt some additional assumptions; cf. the discussion in the PN context in section
3.3
. In principle,
F^{(0)}
and
F^{(1)}
can be computed from the local first- and second-order self-forces acting on the particle
[
138
]
, and the integral at 1PA order is given by

\mathcal{U}_{l0}^{\text{(mem)}}=\int_{0}^{\Omega(U)}\frac{\dot{\mathcal{U}}_{l0}^{\text{(mem)}}}{\dot{\Omega}}\text{d}\Omega^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\Omega(U)}\frac{\dot{\mathcal{U}}_{l0}^{\text{(mem)}}}{F^{(0)}(\Omega^{\prime})+\epsilon F^{(1)}(\Omega^{\prime})}\text{d}\Omega^{\prime}

(61)

without additional assumptions or approximations. The relationship between orbital frequency
\Omega
and retarded time
U
at future null infinity depends on the choice of slicing
\uptau
, as different foliations will connect a time at future null infinity to different points on the particle’s worldline. However, the choice of slicing also affects
F^{(1)}
through the foliation-dependent source term
\delta G^{(1),m}_{\alpha\beta}
in the field equation (
55b
). These two effects cancel out, such that the function
\mathcal{U}_{l0}^{\text{(mem)}}(U)
is in fact insensitive to the choice of slicing (up to 2PA differences).

Unfortunately, at the time of writing, the local second-order self force has not yet been computed.
As a consequence, the forcing functions
F^{(n)}
are instead obtained from an energy-balance law.
For a given choice of time variable
\uptau
, we define a binding energy

\mathcal{E}=M_{\mathrm{B}}-m_{1}-m_{2},

(62)

where the Bondi mass
M_{\mathrm{B}}
and the black hole mass
m_{1}
are evaluated, respectively, on the cuts of future null infinity and of the future horizon defined by
\uptau=\text{constant}
. Note that here we work with the physical black hole mass

m_{1}\equiv\mathring{m}_{1}+\epsilon\delta m_{1}.

(63)

\mathcal{E}
can be calculated as a function of
\{\Omega(\uptau),m_{1}(\uptau),m_{2},a(\uptau)\}

[
89
,
147
]
, such that differentiating equation (
62
) yields

\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}}{\partial\Omega}\dot{\Omega}+\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}}{\partial m_{1}}{\mathcal{}F}_{\mathcal{}H}+\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}}{\partial a}\dot{a}=-{\mathcal{}F}_{\infty}-{\mathcal{}F}_{\mathcal{}H}\equiv-{\mathcal{}F}.

(64)

On the left-hand side, we have applied the chain rule. On the right, we have used the balance laws
dM_{\mathrm{B}}/d\uptau=-\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
and
dm_{1}/d\uptau=\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{}H}
, where
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{}H}
are the flux of energy to future null infinity and the future horizon at time
\uptau
; these flux-balance laws are exact results in GR
[
28
,
148
]
.
We have also used the fact that
m_{2}
is exactly constant to high order in
\epsilon

[
149
]
. Rearranging equation (
64
) for
\dot{\Omega}
, we obtain

\dot{\Omega}=-\frac{{\mathcal{}F}+(\partial{\mathcal{}E}/\partial m_{1}){\mathcal{}F}_{\mathcal{}H}+(\partial\mathcal{E}/\partial a)\dot{a}}{\partial{\mathcal{}E}/\partial\Omega}.

(65)

At this stage we have not made any additional assumptions. We can now expand the binding energy in powers of
\epsilon
,

{\mathcal{}E}=m_{2}\left[{\mathcal{}E}_{(0)}(m_{1}\Omega,a\Omega)-1+\epsilon\,{\mathcal{}E}_{(1)}(m_{1}\Omega,a\Omega)+{\mathcal{}O}(\epsilon^{2})\right],

(66)

where
{\mathcal{}E}_{(0)}
is the specific orbital energy (rather than specific binding energy) of a circular Kerr geodesic, and we have used
M_{\mathrm{B}}=m_{1}+m_{2}{\mathcal{}E}_{(0)}+m_{2}\epsilon\,\mathcal{E}_{(1)}
. Similarly, we expand the fluxes as

{\mathcal{}F}_{\infty/\mathcal{}H}=\epsilon^{2}{\mathcal{}F}^{(1)}_{\infty/\mathcal{}H}(m_{1}\Omega,a\Omega)+\epsilon^{3}{\mathcal{}F}^{(2)}_{\infty/\mathcal{}H}(m_{1}\Omega,a\Omega)+{\mathcal{}O}(\epsilon^{4}),

(67)

where
{\mathcal{}F}^{(1)}_{\infty/\mathcal{}H}
is calculated from products of
h^{(1),m}_{\alpha\beta}
with itself, and
{\mathcal{}F}^{(2)}_{\infty/\mathcal{}H}
is calculated from products of
h^{(2),m}_{\alpha\beta}
with
h^{(1),m}_{\alpha\beta}
. Fully expanding equation (
65
) in powers of
\epsilon
yields the forcing functions
F^{(n)}
in equation (
51b
) in terms of quantities measurable at infinity and the horizon. However, we now adopt one approximation and one assumption:

\bullet

We neglect 1PA horizon flux terms. This includes the explicit
{\mathcal{}O}(\epsilon^{3})
terms
(\partial{\mathcal{}E}/\partial m_{1}){\mathcal{}F}_{\mathcal{}H}
and
(\partial{\mathcal{}E}/\partial a)\dot{a}
in equation (
65
), as well as the contribution of
{\mathcal{}F}^{(2)}_{\mathcal{}H}
to the total flux
{\mathcal{}F}
. The second-order horizon flux,
{\mathcal{}F}^{(2)}_{\mathcal{}H}
, cannot presently be included as it has not been calculated, but all 1PA horizon flux terms are expected to be numerically small
[
150
]
.

\bullet

We assume that the binding energy
{\mathcal{}E}_{(1)}(m_{1}\Omega,a\Omega)
, as defined by equation (
62
), is equal (at least to a sufficiently good approximation) to the binding energy defined from the first law of binary mechanics
[
151
]
. This assumption is required because the binding energy calculated from equation (
62
) was calculated with a different choice of
\uptau
than the fluxes. Even with these different slicings, the two quantities are numerically very close to one another
[
89
]
, but the difference between them has a potentially significant impact on the GW phasing
[
150
]
.

Given these assumptions, we write
\dot{\Omega}
as

\frac{\text{d}\Omega}{\text{d}t}=-\frac{\mathcal{F}}{\partial\mathcal{E}/\partial\Omega}\,,

(68)

with the understanding that we neglect
{\mathcal{}F}^{(2)}_{\mathcal{}H}
in
{\mathcal{}F}
and use the first-law value of
{\mathcal{}E}_{(1)}
.
We provide more detailed formalism required to calculate the metric perturbation and the associated memory contribution at first and second order in the next sections.

4.2
First-order memory for quasi-circular orbits around a Kerr black hole

To calculate the first-order metric amplitudes,
h^{(1)}_{lm}
, we use the Teukolsky formalism
[
152
,
153
,
154
,
77
]
. In this section, we derive a convenient formula for the memory in terms of the asymptotic amplitudes of the (first order) spin-weight
-2
Weyl scalar
\psi^{(1)}_{4}
,

\psi_{4}^{(1)}=C^{(0)}_{\mu\nu\gamma\delta}[h^{(1)}]n^{\mu}\bar{m}^{\nu}n^{\gamma}\bar{m}^{\delta}=\rho^{4}\sum_{l=2}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-l}^{l}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-5.6712pt\kern 6.06009pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-10.25453pt{-2}\kern 6.06009pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-5.6712pt\kern 6.06009pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-10.25453pt{-2}\kern 6.06009pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-3.36946pt\kern 3.75835pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-6.78612pt{-2}\kern 3.75835pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-2.29565pt\kern 2.68454pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-5.71231pt{-2}\kern 2.68454pt{\ell m\omega}}}}(r)\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-4.90419pt\kern 5.29308pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-9.48752pt{-2}\kern 5.29308pt{\ell m}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-4.90419pt\kern 5.29308pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-9.48752pt{-2}\kern 5.29308pt{\ell m}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-2.8982pt\kern 3.28708pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-6.31485pt{-2}\kern 3.28708pt{\ell m}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-1.95901pt\kern 2.3479pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-5.37567pt{-2}\kern 2.3479pt{\ell m}}}}(\theta,\phi)e^{-im\phi_{p}}.

(69)

Here,
C^{(0)}_{\mu\nu\gamma\delta}[h]
is the linearized Weyl tensor (neglecting
\dot{\Omega}
terms that arise from time derivatives),
n^{\mu}
and
\bar{m}^{\mu}
are legs of the Kinnersley tetrad,
\rho=-1/(r-i\mathring{a}\cos\theta)
, and
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-4.90419pt\kern 5.29308pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-9.48752pt{-2}\kern 5.29308pt{\ell m}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-4.90419pt\kern 5.29308pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-9.48752pt{-2}\kern 5.29308pt{\ell m}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-2.8982pt\kern 3.28708pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-6.31485pt{-2}\kern 3.28708pt{\ell m}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-1.95901pt\kern 2.3479pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-5.37567pt{-2}\kern 2.3479pt{\ell m}}}}
is a spin-weighted spheroidal harmonic
[
77
]
. The frequencies are harmonics of the orbital frequency,
\omega=m\Omega
. The spin parameter appearing in the spheroidal harmonics is the background spin
\mathring{a}
. However, our calculations in a Kerr background are limited to first perturbative order, meaning they can neglect the evolving correction to the spin and set
\mathring{a}=a
.

As mentioned above, we can use
t
rather than
\uptau
as our time coordinate in first-order calculations, in which case
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-5.6712pt\kern 6.06009pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-10.25453pt{-2}\kern 6.06009pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-5.6712pt\kern 6.06009pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-10.25453pt{-2}\kern 6.06009pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-3.36946pt\kern 3.75835pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-6.78612pt{-2}\kern 3.75835pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-2.29565pt\kern 2.68454pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-5.71231pt{-2}\kern 2.68454pt{\ell m\omega}}}}
satisfies the radial Teukolsky equation in the form given in equation (84) of reference
[
77
]
. After obtaining the solution in the
t
-foliation, one can transform to the time coordinate
\uptau=t-\upkappa(r_{\star})
using
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-5.6712pt\kern 6.06009pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-10.25453pt{-2}\kern 6.06009pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-5.6712pt\kern 6.06009pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-10.25453pt{-2}\kern 6.06009pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-3.36946pt\kern 3.75835pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-6.78612pt{-2}\kern 3.75835pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-2.29565pt\kern 2.68454pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-5.71231pt{-2}\kern 2.68454pt{\ell m\omega}}}}\to\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-5.6712pt\kern 6.06009pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-10.25453pt{-2}\kern 6.06009pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-5.6712pt\kern 6.06009pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-10.25453pt{-2}\kern 6.06009pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-3.36946pt\kern 3.75835pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-6.78612pt{-2}\kern 3.75835pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-2.29565pt\kern 2.68454pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-5.71231pt{-2}\kern 2.68454pt{\ell m\omega}}}}e^{-i\omega\upkappa}

[
138
,
139
]
. Note that such a simple transformation only applies at leading order in
\epsilon
.

We stress that, at first order in our multiscale expansion, the coefficients
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-5.6712pt\kern 6.06009pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-10.25453pt{-2}\kern 6.06009pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-5.6712pt\kern 6.06009pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-10.25453pt{-2}\kern 6.06009pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-3.36946pt\kern 3.75835pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-6.78612pt{-2}\kern 3.75835pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-2.29565pt\kern 2.68454pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-5.71231pt{-2}\kern 2.68454pt{\ell m\omega}}}}
are identical to those sourced by a particle on a precisely circular, nondissipating geodesic with orbital frequency
\Omega
; the evolution of the frequency only affects the field equations at second order. In the geodesic case and in the
t
-foliation, the phase factor
e^{-im\phi_{p}}
becomes
e^{-im\Omega t}
, and the radial Teukolsky equation is insensitive to this replacement. Therefore, when calculating the
m\neq 0
, oscillatory waveform amplitudes
h^{(1)}_{lm}
, one can either consider geodesic, circular source orbits or evolving, quasi-circular source orbits.

The waveform amplitudes
h^{(1)}_{lm}
themselves are straightforwardly extracted from
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-5.6712pt\kern 6.06009pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-10.25453pt{-2}\kern 6.06009pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-5.6712pt\kern 6.06009pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-10.25453pt{-2}\kern 6.06009pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-3.36946pt\kern 3.75835pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-6.78612pt{-2}\kern 3.75835pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-2.29565pt\kern 2.68454pt{(1)}}}_{{\kern-5.71231pt{-2}\kern 2.68454pt{\ell m\omega}}}}
. Writing the Weyl tensor in terms of
h
, projecting it onto the relevant tetrad legs, and taking the limit as
r\rightarrow\infty
,
one finds that most of the terms in the Weyl scalar drop out, leaving

\psi_{4}=-\frac{1}{2}\ddot{h}_{\bar{m}\bar{m}}=-\frac{1}{2}(\ddot{h}_{+}-i\ddot{h}_{\times}),\mskip 5.0mu plus 5.0mu\mskip 5.0mu plus 5.0mu\mskip 5.0mu plus 5.0mu\mskip 5.0mu plus 5.0mu\mskip 5.0mu plus 5.0mur\rightarrow\infty;

(70)

this holds fully nonlinearly, not restricted to leading order. At first order, the field equation is homogeneous at all points away from the particle. Since the solution to the homogeneous radial Teukolsky equation (in the
t
-foliation) goes as

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-5.6712pt\kern 6.06009pt{(\mathrm{h}om)}}}_{{\kern-10.25453pt{-2}\kern 6.06009pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-5.6712pt\kern 6.06009pt{(\mathrm{h}om)}}}_{{\kern-10.25453pt{-2}\kern 6.06009pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-3.36946pt\kern 3.75835pt{(\mathrm{h}om)}}}_{{\kern-6.78612pt{-2}\kern 3.75835pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}R^{{\kern-2.29565pt\kern 2.68454pt{(\mathrm{h}om)}}}_{{\kern-5.71231pt{-2}\kern 2.68454pt{\ell m\omega}}}}(r)=r^{3}e^{+i\omega r_{*}},\mskip 5.0mu plus 5.0mu\mskip 5.0mu plus 5.0mu\mskip 5.0mu plus 5.0mu\mskip 5.0mu plus 5.0mu\mskip 5.0mu plus 5.0mur\rightarrow\infty,

(71)

this leaves us with

\psi^{(1)}_{4}=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}Z^{{\kern-5.55pt\kern 5.93889pt{\text{Up}}}}_{{\kern-10.13333pt{-2}\kern 5.93889pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}Z^{{\kern-5.55pt\kern 5.93889pt{\text{Up}}}}_{{\kern-10.13333pt{-2}\kern 5.93889pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}Z^{{\kern-3.30652pt\kern 3.6954pt{\text{Up}}}}_{{\kern-6.72318pt{-2}\kern 3.6954pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}Z^{{\kern-2.25069pt\kern 2.63957pt{\text{Up}}}}_{{\kern-5.66734pt{-2}\kern 2.63957pt{\ell m\omega}}}}e^{-im\phi_{p}(u)}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-4.90419pt\kern 5.29308pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-9.48752pt{-2}\kern 5.29308pt{\ell m}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-4.90419pt\kern 5.29308pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-9.48752pt{-2}\kern 5.29308pt{\ell m}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-2.8982pt\kern 3.28708pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-6.31485pt{-2}\kern 3.28708pt{\ell m}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-1.95901pt\kern 2.3479pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-5.37567pt{-2}\kern 2.3479pt{\ell m}}}}(\theta,\phi),\mskip 5.0mu plus 5.0mu\mskip 5.0mu plus 5.0mu\mskip 5.0mu plus 5.0mu\mskip 5.0mu plus 5.0mu\mskip 5.0mu plus 5.0mur\rightarrow\infty,

(72)

where the Teukolsky amplitude
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}Z^{{\kern-5.55pt\kern 5.93889pt{\text{Up}}}}_{{\kern-10.13333pt{-2}\kern 5.93889pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}Z^{{\kern-5.55pt\kern 5.93889pt{\text{Up}}}}_{{\kern-10.13333pt{-2}\kern 5.93889pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}Z^{{\kern-3.30652pt\kern 3.6954pt{\text{Up}}}}_{{\kern-6.72318pt{-2}\kern 3.6954pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}Z^{{\kern-2.25069pt\kern 2.63957pt{\text{Up}}}}_{{\kern-5.66734pt{-2}\kern 2.63957pt{\ell m\omega}}}}
is an
\Omega
-dependent constant (determined by solving the inhomogeneous Teukolsky equation and reading off the coefficient of
1/r
at large
r
), and we have cancelled the factor
e^{+i\omega r_{*}}
by transforming to the
\uptau
-foliation.
Integrating with respect to
u
leaves us with

\dot{h}^{(1)}=\frac{2}{r}\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\frac{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}Z^{{\kern-5.55pt\kern 5.93889pt{\text{Up}}}}_{{\kern-10.13333pt{-2}\kern 5.93889pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}Z^{{\kern-5.55pt\kern 5.93889pt{\text{Up}}}}_{{\kern-10.13333pt{-2}\kern 5.93889pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}Z^{{\kern-3.30652pt\kern 3.6954pt{\text{Up}}}}_{{\kern-6.72318pt{-2}\kern 3.6954pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}Z^{{\kern-2.25069pt\kern 2.63957pt{\text{Up}}}}_{{\kern-5.66734pt{-2}\kern 2.63957pt{\ell m\omega}}}}}{im\Omega}e^{-im\phi_{p}(u)}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-4.90419pt\kern 5.29308pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-9.48752pt{-2}\kern 5.29308pt{\ell m}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-4.90419pt\kern 5.29308pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-9.48752pt{-2}\kern 5.29308pt{\ell m}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-2.8982pt\kern 3.28708pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-6.31485pt{-2}\kern 3.28708pt{\ell m}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-1.95901pt\kern 2.3479pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-5.37567pt{-2}\kern 2.3479pt{\ell m}}}}(\theta,\phi).

(73)

A second integration yields

h^{(1)}=\frac{2}{r}\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\frac{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}Z^{{\kern-5.55pt\kern 5.93889pt{\text{Up}}}}_{{\kern-10.13333pt{-2}\kern 5.93889pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}Z^{{\kern-5.55pt\kern 5.93889pt{\text{Up}}}}_{{\kern-10.13333pt{-2}\kern 5.93889pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}Z^{{\kern-3.30652pt\kern 3.6954pt{\text{Up}}}}_{{\kern-6.72318pt{-2}\kern 3.6954pt{\ell m\omega}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}Z^{{\kern-2.25069pt\kern 2.63957pt{\text{Up}}}}_{{\kern-5.66734pt{-2}\kern 2.63957pt{\ell m\omega}}}}}{(m\Omega)^{2}}e^{-im\phi_{p}(u)}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-4.90419pt\kern 5.29308pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-9.48752pt{-2}\kern 5.29308pt{\ell m}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-4.90419pt\kern 5.29308pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-9.48752pt{-2}\kern 5.29308pt{\ell m}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-2.8982pt\kern 3.28708pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-6.31485pt{-2}\kern 3.28708pt{\ell m}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-1.95901pt\kern 2.3479pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-5.37567pt{-2}\kern 2.3479pt{\ell m}}}}(\theta,\phi).

(74)

These integrations are performed in the manner of equation (
58
), neglecting all subleading terms. Such terms are automatically accounted for in the second-order metric amplitudes.

Calculations in the Teukolsky formalism in Kerr spacetime are done in a spheroidal harmonic basis. To facilitate analytical integrations
over the combinations of harmonics appearing in, e.g., equation (
10
), and in keeping with the expressions shown in section
2
,
we must project these results onto a basis of spherical harmonics. This has the fortunate side effect of allowing easier comparisons with PN and NR. The expansion
is written as

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-4.90419pt\kern 5.29308pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-9.48752pt{-2}\kern 5.29308pt{\ell m}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-4.90419pt\kern 5.29308pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-9.48752pt{-2}\kern 5.29308pt{\ell m}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-2.8982pt\kern 3.28708pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-6.31485pt{-2}\kern 3.28708pt{\ell m}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2}}}}S^{{\kern-1.95901pt\kern 2.3479pt{a\omega}}}_{{\kern-5.37567pt{-2}\kern 2.3479pt{\ell m}}}}=\sum_{l=l_{\text{min}}}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2\!}}}}Y^{{\kern-6.03407pt\kern 6.42296pt}}_{{\kern-9.80075pt{-2\!}\kern 6.42296pt{lm}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2\!}}}}Y^{{\kern-6.03407pt\kern 6.42296pt}}_{{\kern-9.80075pt{-2\!}\kern 6.42296pt{lm}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2\!}}}}Y^{{\kern-3.54472pt\kern 3.93361pt}}_{{\kern-6.37805pt{-2\!}\kern 3.93361pt{lm}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{-2\!}}}}Y^{{\kern-2.42082pt\kern 2.80971pt}}_{{\kern-5.25415pt{-2\!}\kern 2.80971pt{lm}}}}\mskip 5.0mu plus 5.0mub_{\ell lm}^{a\omega},

(75)

where the
b
coefficients can be obtained following appendix A of
[
155
]
, and
l_{\text{min}}=\ell_{\text{min}}=\text{max}(m,2)
.
This allows us to write

h^{(1)}_{lm}=\sum_{\ell=\ell_{\text{min}}}^{\infty}h^{(1)}_{\ell m}b_{\ell lm}^{a\omega},

(76)

and by considering the form of
h_{\ell m}
we can further define

Z^{(1)}_{lm}=\sum_{\ell=\ell_{\text{min}}}^{\infty}{}_{-2}Z^{\mathrm{U}p}_{\ell m\omega}\,b_{\ell lm}^{a\omega}.

(77)

Using these expressions, we can express equation (
73
) in a spherical basis by making the swap
\ell\rightarrow l
, and replacing each
spin-weighted spheroidal harmonic with the corresponding spin-weighted spherical harmonic. In the Schwarzschild case, where
\mathring{a}=0
, we get that

b_{\ell lm}^{0}=\delta_{\ell l}
because spheroidal harmonics reduce to spherical ones in this case, and no projection is necessary. In either case, in the expansion (
56
), we have

h^{(1)}_{lm}=\frac{2Z^{(1)}_{lm}}{(m\Omega)^{2}}.

(78)

Substituting
h
in the above form into equation (
14
), we can write the time derivative of the radiative mass moment as

\dot{\mathcal{U}}_{lm}^{\text{(1,mem)}}=\sqrt{\frac{2(l-2)!}{(l+2)!}}\sum_{l^{\prime}=2}^{\infty}\sum_{l^{\prime\prime}=2}^{\infty}\sum_{m^{\prime}=-l^{\prime}}^{l^{\prime}}\sum_{m^{\prime\prime}=-l^{\prime\prime}}^{l^{\prime\prime}}\frac{(-4)(-1)^{m+m^{\prime\prime}}}{m^{\prime}m^{\prime\prime}\Omega^{2}}\\
\times Z^{(1)}_{l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}Z^{(1)*}_{l^{\prime\prime}m^{\prime\prime}}e^{-i(m^{\prime}-m^{\prime\prime})\phi_{p}(u)}G^{2,-2,0}_{l^{\prime}\!,\,l^{\prime\prime}\!,\,l,m^{\prime}\!,\,-m^{\prime\prime}\!,\,-m}\,.

(79)

Finally, we reduce the summation using several symmetries. We eliminate the sum over
m^{\prime\prime}
using the fact that
G^{2,-2,0}_{l^{\prime},l^{\prime\prime},l,m^{\prime},-m^{\prime\prime},-m}
vanishes unless
m^{\prime}-m^{\prime\prime}=m
, by virtue of the 3-
j
symbols in equation (
16
). We restrict to positive
m^{\prime}
by using the symmetry
Z^{(1)}_{lm}=(-1)^{l}Z^{(1)*}_{l-m}
. Also restricting ourselves to the non-oscillatory
m=0
modes, we obtain

\dot{\mathcal{U}}_{l0}^{\text{(1,mem)}}=-8\sqrt{\frac{2(l-2)!}{(l+2)!}}\sum_{l^{\prime}=2}^{\infty}\sum_{l^{\prime\prime}=2}^{\infty}\sum_{m^{\prime}=1}^{l^{\prime}}\frac{(-1)^{m^{\prime}}}{(m^{\prime}\Omega)^{2}}\Re\!\left(Z^{(1)}_{l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}Z^{(1)*}_{l^{\prime\prime}m^{\prime}}\right)G^{2,-2,0}_{l^{\prime}\!,\,l^{\prime\prime}\!,\,l,m^{\prime}\!,\,-m^{\prime}\!,\,0}\ ,

(80)

where we have additionally used the symmetry (
17
). Note that by virtue of the system’s up-down symmetry,
\dot{\mathcal{U}}_{l0}
vanishes for odd values of
l
.

We calculate the accumulated memory over the inspiral using equation (
61
). The evolution
of the frequency is governed by equation (
51b
), which can equivalently be rewritten in terms of energy as equation (
68
). At leading (0PA) order, we have

\dot{\Omega}=\epsilon F^{(0)}(\Omega)=-\frac{\epsilon{\mathcal{}F}^{(1)}(\Omega)}{\partial{\mathcal{}E}_{(0)}/\partial\Omega},

(81)

where
{\mathcal{}E}_{(0)}
is the specific energy of a circular geodesic
[
156
]
,

\mathcal{E}_{(0)}=\frac{1-2(m_{1}\Omega)^{2/3}(1-a\Omega)^{1/3}}{\sqrt{1-(a\Omega)^{2}-3(m_{1}\Omega)^{2/3}(1-a\Omega)^{4/3}}},

(82)

and
\mathcal{F}^{(1)}
is the instantaneous flux of energy (per unit mass squared) carried by
h^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
into the black hole and out to infinity. This flux is readily calculated from the Teukolsky mode amplitudes at infinity and the black hole horizon
[
77
]
. Unlike the 1PA balance law, the 0PA balance law (
81
) is known to hold exactly and is independent of one’s choice of
\uptau

[
138
]
. We also note that if calculations are restricted to 0PA order, as ours are in Kerr, then we can everywhere replace
\mathring{m}_{1}
and
\mathring{a}
with
m_{1}
and
a
and treat them as constants (though the horizon flux
{\mathcal{}F}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{}H}
must be included in
{\mathcal{}F}^{(1)}
).

4.3
Second-order memory for quasi-circular orbits around a Schwarzschild black hole

We now turn to the computation of the memory at second order in the mass ratio. In this section, we develop a simple extension of the formula (
80
) in terms of a certain effective second-order Teukolsky amplitude.

Our calculation of the second-order memory begins from the metric amplitudes
h^{(2)}_{lm}
that were computed in Schwarzschild in reference
[
90
]
. Rather than involving the Teukolsky formalism, these were computed by directly solving the field equations (
55a
) and (
55b
) in the Lorenz gauge, as described in Refs.
[
157
,
138
,
139
]
and utilizing the ingredients from Refs.
[
146
,
137
,
158
,
159
]
. At second order and beyond, the relationship between the orbital frequency
\Omega
and the asymptotic metric amplitudes depends on the choice of time foliation. The calculations in reference
[
90
]
specifically use a “sharp slicing”
[
139
]
in which
\uptau=t
in a shell
r_{-}<r_{(0)}<r_{+}
centered on the particle’s leading-order (evolving) orbital radius
r_{(0)}
;
\uptau=v
in a region extending from the shell to the horizon; and
\uptau=u
from the shell to infinity.

The GW memory is calculated from
\dot{h}
, which we can obtain from a time derivative of equation (
56
),

\displaystyle\dot{h}=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{lm}\left[\epsilon(-im\Omega)h^{(1)}_{lm}+\epsilon^{2}(-im\Omega)h^{(2)}_{lm}+\epsilon^{2}F^{(0)}\partial_{\Omega}h^{(1)}_{lm}\right]e^{-im\phi_{p}}\,{}_{-2}Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi).

(83)

Here we have accounted for the time derivative of the amplitude
h^{(1)}_{lm}
and used equation (
51b
). We can work directly with this formula, but it will be convenient to define an effective second-order Teukolsky amplitude
Z^{(2)}_{lm}
that allows us to easily extend the first-order expression (
80
):

\mathchoice{Z^{{{(2)}}}_{{{lm}}}}{Z^{{{(2)}}}_{{{lm}}}}{Z^{{{(2)}}}_{{{lm}}}}{Z^{{{(2)}}}_{{{lm}}}}\equiv\frac{1}{2}im\Omega\left(-im\Omega h^{(2)}_{lm}+F^{(0)}\partial_{\Omega}h^{(1)}_{lm}\right)=\frac{1}{2}(m\Omega)^{2}\left(h^{(2)}_{lm}+\frac{iF^{(0)}}{m\Omega}\partial_{\Omega}h^{(1)}_{lm}\right).

(84)

This amplitude is what would arise by ignoring
\dot{\Omega}
terms when taking the second time derivative in equation (
70
). Since the derivation at first order assumed
d/du=-im\Omega
when relating
\dot{h}
to
\psi_{4}
, we can immediately extend equation (
80
) to second order by making the simple substitution

Z^{(1)}_{lm}\rightarrow Z_{lm}^{(1)}+\epsilon\,Z_{lm}^{(2)}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2}),

(85)

and reading off the terms linear in
\epsilon
. We then write the total
\dot{\mathcal{U}}_{l0}^{\text{(mem)}}
as

\dot{\mathcal{U}}_{l0}^{\text{(mem)}}=\epsilon^{2}\left[\dot{\mathcal{U}}_{l0}^{\text{(1, mem)}}+\epsilon\,\dot{\mathcal{U}}_{l0}^{\text{(2, mem)}}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\right],

(86)

with
\dot{\mathcal{U}}_{l0}^{\text{(2, mem)}}
given by equation (
80
) with the replacement

\Re\!\left(Z^{(1)}_{l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}Z^{(1)*}_{l^{\prime\prime}m^{\prime}}\right)\to\Re\!\left(Z^{(1)}_{l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}Z^{(2)*}_{l^{\prime\prime}m^{\prime}}+Z^{(2)}_{l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}Z^{(1)*}_{l^{\prime\prime}m^{\prime}}\right).

(87)

An equivalent formula for
\dot{\mathcal{U}}_{l0}^{\text{(2, mem)}}
can readily be obtained in terms of the physical first-and second-order Teukolsky amplitudes (as opposed to the effective
Z^{(2)}_{lm}
). To do so, one would (i) take a time derivative of equation (
83
) and include all
\dot{\Omega}
terms, (ii) use equation (
70
) to equate the physical second-order Teukolsky amplitude to terms in the derivative of equation (
83
), and (iii) integrate over
u
as in equation (
58
) (including the first subleading term) to obtain the second-order piece of
\dot{h}
in terms of the physical Teukolsky amplitudes.

5
Spacetime asymptotics and hereditary effects in the self-force regime

In the preceding section, we have implicitly assumed that solving equation (
55
) gives us direct access to the asymptotic waveform amplitudes
h^{(1)}_{lm}
and
h^{(2)}_{lm}
. At first order in SF theory, this assumption is correct: we can obtain
h^{(1)}_{lm}
simply by evaluating
h^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
at
r\to\infty
(at fixed
u
). If
h^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
is calculated in a gauge that is asymptotically irregular, such as the Regge-Wheeler-Zerilli gauge, then there are standard prescriptions for transforming to a gauge that extends smoothly to future null infinity; see, e.g., reference
[
160
]
for the case of the Regge-Wheeler-Zerilli gauge. If
h^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
is calculated in the Lorenz gauge, no transformation is required.

At second order in perturbation theory, extracting the waveform becomes significantly more difficult due to an interplay between the multiscale expansion and gauge choice. As explored in reference
[
137
]
, in a gauge in which the linearized Einstein tensor reduces to a wave operator (as it does in the Lorenz gauge), the field equation (
55b
) suffers from logarithmic infrared divergences stemming from the source terms’ slow falloff at large distances.

The infrared breakdown confines the multiscale expansion to the near zone defined by
r\ll M/\epsilon
(note this definition of the near zone is not related to the wavelength of the emitted radiation, unlike the definition used in the PN-MPM construction). To overcome the breakdown, we introduce a weak-field, PM expansion in the far zone
r\gg M
, inspired by the MPM formalism. Information is transmitted between the multiscale expansion and the PM expansion in a buffer region (the ‘near far zone’
M\ll r\ll M/\epsilon
) where both approximations are valid, corresponding to the large-
r
limit of the near zone and the small-
r
limit of the far zone. Re-expanding the far-zone PM solution in this buffer region provides physical boundary conditions for the multiscale solution.

We illustrate the breakdown of the multiscale expansion in section
5.1
, and we outline the PM expansion in section
5.2
. We then apply the formalism to show that

(i)

matching to the far-zone solution introduces hereditary, nonlocal-in-time effects in the near-zone dynamics, which cannot otherwise be captured by the multiscale expansion,

(ii)

the hereditary near-zone effects arise from precisely the same piece of the metric that corresponds to memory in the GW,

(iii)

‘most’ of the oscillatory part of the waveform at true asymptotic infinity, in the very far zone
r\gg M/\epsilon
, can be directly extracted from a certain piece of the multiscale solution in the large-
r
limit of the near zone; this part of the metric propagates undistorted from the near zone to the very far zone.

(iv)

additional oscillatory modes with hereditary amplitudes arise from products between first-order memory terms and first-order oscillatory terms. The impact of these new modes will require further investigation.

The first three of these are in close analogy with classic results of the PN-MPM construction
[
80
,
9
]
. However, we note that in the PN-MPM calculations, both tails and memory effects give rise to nonlocal-in-time integrals, while in our context, nonlocal-in-time integrals are always associated with memory-type, low-frequency effects. Ordinary tails, which involve oscillatory integrands, are always reduced to local-in-time functions by virtue of equations such as (
58
).

The framework we describe in this section underlies the 2SF results in references
[
89
,
90
,
91
]
. Here we describe it and its links to GW memory for the first time.
To clearly split between quasistationary and oscillatory effects, in this section we introduce the notation

f(u)=\left\langle f(u)\right\rangle+f^{\mathrm{o}sc.}(u)

(88)

for functions
f(u)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{m}(\Omega(u))e^{-im\phi_{p}(u)}
, where

\left\langle f\right\rangle=f_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad f^{\mathrm{o}sc.}=\sum_{m\neq 0}f_{m}e^{-im\phi_{p}}.

(89)

5.1
Breakdown of the multiscale expansion

As context for our analysis in the next sections, we first review the infrared breakdown of the multiscale expansion. This breakdown, as mentioned above, stems from the large-
r
behavior of the source terms in equation (
55b
). Since the problem arises from large-
r
behavior, our illustration in this section will focus on the large-
r
limit of our multiscale field equations.

If the
t
-foliation is used, then the first-order solution behaves as
h^{(1),m}_{\alpha\beta}\sim\frac{e^{im\Omega r_{*}}}{r}
at large
r
, meaning the source
\delta G^{(1),m}_{\alpha\beta}[h^{(1),m}]
behaves particularly poorly. A second
t
-derivative in the orginal four-dimensional
\delta G_{\alpha\beta}
gives rise to terms like
\Omega\dot{\Omega}\partial_{\Omega}
, leading to

\delta G^{(1),m}_{\alpha\beta}[h^{(1),m}]\sim\Omega F^{(0)}\partial_{\Omega}h^{(1),m}_{\alpha\beta}\sim\Omega F^{(0)}\partial_{\Omega}\frac{e^{im\Omega r_{*}}}{r}=im\Omega F^{(0)}\frac{r_{*}e^{im\Omega r_{*}}}{r}.

(90)

This is immediately cured by using hyperboloidal time
\uptau
in the multiscale expansion
[
137
,
138
,
139
]
, which eliminates the exponential
e^{im\Omega r_{*}}
(and hence eliminates the
\Omega
derivative of it) as well as eliminating second time derivatives from
\delta G_{\alpha\beta}
(and hence replacing
\Omega\dot{\Omega}\partial_{\Omega}
terms in
\delta G^{(1),m}_{\alpha\beta}
with
\dot{\Omega}\partial_{r}\partial_{\Omega}
terms).

A more pernicious problem arises from the quadratic source term
\delta^{2}G^{(0),m}_{\alpha\beta}
. In a generic gauge and the
t
-foliation, this decays as the product of two oscillating GWs,

\delta^{2}G^{(0),m}_{\alpha\beta}\sim\sum_{m^{\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}m^{\prime}m^{\prime\prime}\Omega^{2}h^{(1),m^{\prime}}h^{(1),m^{\prime\prime}}\sim\frac{e^{im\Omega r_{\star}}}{r^{2}}.

(91)

In hyperboloidal slicing, the exponential
e^{im\Omega r_{\star}}
is removed but the falloff is otherwise unchanged. We will explain the consequences of this by adapting an analysis from reference
[
137
]
(see also section VI of
[
161
]
). First, recall that in the multiscale framework, after
e^{-im\phi_{p}}
is factored out, the field equations only involve spatial derivatives and functions of
\Omega
. For example, at large
r
in
t
slicing, the field equation (
55b
) in the Lorenz gauge reduces to
8
8

8

cf. the exact form of the Lorenz-gauge linearized Einstein tensor, (
103
) below.

\Box^{(0)}_{\eta}h^{(2),m}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{e^{im\Omega r}}{r^{2}}s^{m}_{\alpha\beta}(\Omega,\theta)e^{im\phi},\hskip 25.0ptr\to\infty,

(92)

where
s^{m}_{\alpha\beta}e^{im\phi}
is the coefficient of
e^{im\Omega r_{\star}}/r^{2}
in the large-
r
expansion of the quadratic source, and
\Box^{(0)}_{\eta}=(m\Omega)^{2}+\vec{\nabla}^{2}
is the flat-space d’Alembert operator with
\partial_{t}=-im\Omega
. One solves each such field equation at fixed
\Omega
. We can hence formally define the retarded solution to equation (
55b
) as the integral of the source against a retarded Green’s function
G_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}(\omega,x^{i},x^{\prime i})
for the frequency-domain linearised Einstein equation. The contribution from the source’s large-
r
tail, as written in equation (
92
), is then

h^{(2),m}_{\alpha\beta}(x^{i})=\int dr^{\prime}\int d\Omega^{\prime}_{2}\,G_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}(\omega,x^{i},x^{\prime i})e^{im\Omega r^{\prime}}s^{m}_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}(\Omega,\theta^{\prime})e^{im\phi^{\prime}}+\ldots,

(93)

where the Green’s function is evaluated at
\omega=m\Omega
and we have cancelled the
r^{-2}
in the source with the
r^{2}
in the volume element. The ellipses indicate that this formula only represents the contribution of the large-
r
behavior of the source.

Since we are considering the behavior at large
r
coming from the source at large
r^{\prime}
, we can approximate
G_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}
as its flat-spacetime limit,

G_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\delta_{\alpha}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta}^{\beta^{\prime}}\frac{e^{i\omega|\vec{x}-\vec{x}^{\prime}|}}{|\vec{x}-\vec{x}^{\prime}|},

(94)

which satisfies

(\omega^{2}+\vec{\nabla}^{2})G_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}=\delta_{\alpha}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta}^{\beta^{\prime}}\delta^{3}(\vec{x}-\vec{x}^{\prime}).

(95)

Here
\vec{x}=(x,y,z)
are Cartesian coordinates and we work with Cartesian components. Expanded in scalar spherical harmonics, for large
r
and
r^{\prime}
, and for
\omega\neq 0
modes, this becomes

G_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\approx-\frac{1}{2i\omega rr^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta}^{\beta^{\prime}}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m^{\prime}=-l}^{l}\left[(-1)^{l}e^{i\omega r}-e^{-i\omega r}\right]e^{i\omega r^{\prime}}Y^{*}_{lm^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})Y_{lm^{\prime}}(\theta,\phi)

(96)

for
r^{\prime}>r
(and
r\leftrightarrow r^{\prime}
if
r>r^{\prime})
; cf. equation (14) of
[
158
]
. For
\omega=0
, the dominant behavior is confined to
l=0
, and the Green’s function reduces to

G_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\approx-\frac{1}{4\pi}\delta_{\alpha}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta}^{\beta^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\theta(r-r^{\prime})}{r}+\frac{\theta(r^{\prime}-r)}{r^{\prime}}\right);

(97)

cf. equation (15) of
[
158
]
.

First, consider
\omega\neq 0
modes. The approximations (
93
) and (
96
) imply

h^{(2),m}_{\alpha\beta}\approx-\frac{1}{2i\omega r}\sum_{l\geq|m|}Y_{lm}\left[e^{i\omega r}\int^{r}_{R_{0}}\frac{\beta_{l}(\omega r^{\prime})e^{i\omega r^{\prime}}}{r^{\prime}}dr^{\prime}+\beta_{l}(\omega r)\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{e^{2i\omega r^{\prime}}}{r^{\prime}}dr^{\prime}\right]s^{lm}_{\alpha\beta}(\Omega),

(98)

where for convenience we have introduced
\beta_{l}(\omega r)=\left[(-1)^{l}e^{i\omega r}-e^{-i\omega r}\right]
. We have set an arbitrary (large) lower limit
R_{0}<r
for the radial integral; this is freely done in the present illustration because we are only interested in the contribution of the large-
r
source. Also note that the integration over
\phi^{\prime}
has imposed
m^{\prime}=m
. The radial integral from
r
to
\infty
converges and yields a subdominant term of order
1/r^{2}
in
h^{(2,m)}_{\alpha\beta}
. The first term in the integral from
R_{0}
to
r
,
\int_{R_{0}}^{r}dr^{\prime}\,{e^{2i\omega r^{\prime}}}/{r^{\prime}}
, does likewise (at leading order for large
R_{0}
). However, the second term in that integral contributes a logarithm:
\int_{R_{0}}^{r}dr^{\prime}\,{(-1)^{l+1}}/{r^{\prime}}=(-1)^{l+1}\ln(r/R_{0})
. Therefore, for
m\neq 0
modes, the retarded integral converges but yields the irregular, logarithmic asymptotic behavior

h^{(2),m}_{\alpha\beta}\approx\frac{\ln(r)e^{im\Omega r}}{2im\Omega r}\sum_{l\geq|m|}s^{lm}_{\alpha\beta}(\Omega)Y_{lm},

(99)

mirroring well-known behavior in harmonic coordinates
[
107
]
. Here we have discarded the arbitrary radius
R_{0}
, which will not appear in the actual retarded integral using the fully relativistic source and Green’s function; that integral will run from the horizon to infinity and involve no arbitrary constants.

Next, consider
\omega=0
modes. The approximations (
93
) and (
97
) imply

h^{(2),0}_{\alpha\beta}\approx-\left(\frac{1}{r}\int^{r}_{R_{0}}dr^{\prime}+\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{dr^{\prime}}{r^{\prime}}\right)s^{00}_{\alpha\beta}(\Omega)Y_{00}\,.

(100)

The first integral contributes a constant term
\propto r^{0}
in
h^{(2,0)}_{\alpha\beta}
, making it already asymptotically irregular. The second integral does not converge at all. Its logarithmic divergence is the principal signature of the breakdown of the multiscale expansion. Note that here it occurs in the
l=0
mode, but this is an artefact of having expanded Cartesian components in scalar spherical harmonics. What appears as a pure
l=0
mode in this expansion would contribute to
0\leq l\leq 2
modes in an expansion of tetrad components in spin-weighted spherical harmonics (while remaining confined to
m=0
).

We have performed the analysis in this section in
t
slicing for simplicity, but the results are the same in
\uptau
slicing; the only effect is to remove the exponential factor from equation (
99
). We refer to
[
137
]
for a more detailed analysis in the case of a nonlinear scalar toy model.

Before proceeding, we comment on an underlying cause of this breakdown: in a generic gauge,
u={\mathrm{c}onstant}
is not a light cone of the perturbed spacetime (cf. historical references given on p. 386 of
[
107
]
). As highlighted in reference
[
162
]
, this can be cured by transforming
h^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
to a Bondi-Sachs gauge
[
28
]
, which enforces that
u={\mathrm{c}onstant}
is an outgoing null cone in both the background and perturbed spacetime. If we were to transform
h^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
to a Bondi-Sachs gauge before constructing the source for the second-order field equations, then certain components of the quadratic source term in the field equations would decay more rapidly at large
r
. This suffices to eliminate infrared divergences in the retarded integral for the second-order Teukolsky equation
[
162
]
, though the impact of this gauge choice on the full second-order Einstein field equations has not been fully investigated.

In principle, one could follow a three-step procedure: (i) solve for
h^{(1),m}_{\alpha\beta}
in any convenient gauge, (ii) transform
h^{(1),m}_{\alpha\beta}
to a Bondi-Sachs gauge and construct the source terms in equation (
55b
) from it, and (iii) impose any convenient gauge for
h^{(2),m}_{\alpha\beta}
when solving equation (
55b
). This strategy, which would mirror an analogous approach in reference
[
121
]
, will be further developed in a sequel paper
[
163
]
. For the remainder of this section, we describe instead the strategy used in
[
89
]
and
[
90
]
, which will also allow us to directly relate our calculations to the derivation of GW memory due to Blanchet and Damour
[
9
]
.

5.2
Post-Minkowskian expansion in the far zone
r\gg M

We cure the multiscale expansion’s large-
r
pathology by adopting a far-zone, MPM-like expansion based on reference
[
137
]
. The essential point in this construction is that we solve the field equations directly on spacetime rather than treating
(\phi_{p},\Omega)
as independent variables, using four-dimensional retarded integrals rather than three-dimensional, fixed-frequency retarded integrals. Evaluating the retarded integrals is made tractable by working in the PM limit outside the source region, at
r\gg M
, meaning we solve flat-spacetime wave equations rather than curved-spacetime ones.

Our starting point is the self-consistent formulation of the small-
\epsilon
expansion
[
164
,
87
]
, as extended in reference
[
138
]
. This formulation is valid over the entire spacetime, in both the near zone and the far zone. When the self-consistent formulation is re-expanded in multiscale form, it recovers the multiscale formulation in the near zone
[
138
]
. In this section, we expand it in a PM form valid in the far zone.

In the self-consistent framework, we treat
h_{\alpha\beta}
as a function of spacetime coordinates, of the particle’s mass and the background black hole parameters, and of a set of matter variables
\Psi(t,\epsilon)=\{\gamma(t,\epsilon),\delta m_{1}(t,\epsilon),\delta a(t,\epsilon)\}
comprising the particle’s trajectory
\gamma
and the black hole’s small, evolving mass and spin corrections. We then define an expansion in powers of the mass ratio at fixed spacetime coordinates and fixed
\Psi
:

h_{\alpha\beta}=\epsilon h^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta}(x^{\mu};\Psi)+\epsilon^{2}h^{[2]}_{\alpha\beta}(x^{\mu};\Psi)+\ldots,

(101)

where we use square brackets to distinguish orders in this expansion from orders in the multiscale expansion. As we did when solving the multiscale-expanded field equations through second order, we adopt the Lorenz gauge condition, which we now state explicitly:

\mathring{g}^{\alpha\beta}\mathring{\nabla}_{\alpha}\bar{h}_{\beta\gamma}=0\,,

(102)

where
\bar{h}_{\alpha\beta}\equiv h_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\mathring{g}_{\alpha\beta}\mathring{g}^{\mu\nu}h_{\mu\nu}
and
\mathring{\nabla}_{\alpha}
is the covariant derivative compatible with
\mathring{g}_{\alpha\beta}
. With this gauge choice, the linearized Einstein tensor reduces to a wave operator,

\delta G_{\alpha\beta}[h]=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathring{\Box}\bar{h}_{\alpha\beta}+2\mathring{R}_{\alpha}{}^{\mu}{}_{\beta}{}^{\nu}\bar{h}_{\mu\nu}\right)\equiv-\frac{1}{2}E_{\alpha\beta}[\bar{h}]\,,

(103)

where
\mathring{\Box}=\mathring{g}^{\alpha\beta}\mathring{\nabla}_{\alpha}\mathring{\nabla}_{\beta}
and where
\mathring{R}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
is the Riemann tensor associated with the background metric
\mathring{g}_{\alpha\beta}
. We can write a sequence of field equations for each
h^{[n]}_{\alpha\beta}
,

\displaystyle E_{\alpha\beta}[\bar{h}^{[1]}]

\displaystyle=-16\pi T^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta}\,,

(104a)

\displaystyle E_{\alpha\beta}[\bar{h}^{[2]}]

\displaystyle=-16\pi T^{[2]}_{\alpha\beta}+2\delta^{2}G_{\alpha\beta}[h^{[1]},h^{[1]}]\,.

(104b)

These are coupled to the equation for the particle’s self-forced trajectory
x^{\alpha}_{p}
,

\mathring{u}^{\beta}\mathring{\nabla}_{\!\beta}\mathring{u}^{\alpha}=\epsilon\delta f^{\alpha}[h^{[1]}]+\epsilon^{2}\left(\delta f^{\alpha}[h^{[2]}]+\delta^{2}f^{\alpha}[h^{[1]},h^{[1]}]\right),

(105)

where
\mathring{u}^{\beta}\equiv\frac{dx^{\beta}_{p}}{d\mathring{\tau}}
is the particle’s four-velocity normalized in the background spacetime, and
f^{\alpha}_{[1]}
and
f^{\alpha}_{[2]}
are the linear and quadratic self-forces
[
165
]
. The evolving corrections to the primary’s mass and spin are incorporated into
h^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta}
as described in reference
[
138
]
. The field equations (
104
) and matter equations are to be solved together, self-consistently, as a coupled set. The gauge condition (
102
) is then automatically enforced by the matter evolution equations, so long as initial data satisfies the gauge condition
[
166
]
.

To see why the self-consistent expansion evades the infrared divergences of the multiscale expansion, we can formally write the self-consistent solutions as retarded integrals over all spacetime,

\displaystyle\bar{h}^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta}(x^{\mu})

\displaystyle=-16\pi\int G_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}(x^{\mu},x^{\prime\mu})T^{[1]}_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\sqrt{-\mathring{g}^{\prime}}d^{4}x^{\prime}+\bar{h}^{\delta m_{1},\delta a_{1}}_{\alpha\beta},

(106a)

\displaystyle\bar{h}^{[2]}_{\alpha\beta}(x^{\mu})

\displaystyle=-16\pi\int G_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}(x^{\mu},x^{\prime\mu})\left(T^{[2]}_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{8\pi}\delta^{2}G_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}[h^{[1]},h^{[1]}]\right)\sqrt{-\mathring{g}^{\prime}}d^{4}x^{\prime}.

(106b)

Here
\bar{h}^{\delta m_{1},\delta a_{1}}_{\alpha\beta}
represents the contribution from the primary’s evolving mass and spin corrections, and
G_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}
is the retarded Green’s function for the operator
E_{\alpha\beta}
, satisfying

\mathring{\Box}G_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}+2\mathring{R}_{\alpha}{}^{\mu}{}_{\beta}{}^{\nu}G_{\mu\nu}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}=\delta^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\alpha}\delta^{\beta^{\prime}}_{\beta}\frac{\delta^{4}(x^{\mu}-x^{\prime\mu})}{\sqrt{-\mathring{g}}}.

(107)

Crucially, the integrals (
106
) involve integration over time, and the trajectory (and the primary’s mass and spin) and metric perturbations evolve self-consistently within that integration. This is important because the amplitude of GW content in
h^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta}
decays toward
u=-\infty
(since the binary asymptotes to a Newtonian, nonradiating state in the infinite past). Consequently, the problematic,
1/r^{2}
term in
\delta^{2}G_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}[h^{[1]},h^{[1]}]
also has decaying amplitude toward
u=-\infty
; this can be understood from the fact that the
1/r^{2}
part of
\delta^{2}G_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}[h^{[1]},h^{[1]}]
arises from the product of two GWs, as in equation (
91
). The decay of
\delta^{2}G_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}[h^{[1]},h^{[1]}]
in turn ensures the convergence of the four-dimensional integral (
106b
). In the multiscale solution, on the other hand, the second-order field equation (
55b
) is solved at fixed values of
\Omega
and therefore at fixed values of the GW amplitudes. The solution in that case is given by a three-dimensional integral of the form (
93
) (see reference
[
139
]
for the explicit, fully relativistic construction), which does not naturally capture the quadratic source term’s decay to zero in the distant past. This is analogous to the discussion in Sec.
3.2
in the PN context; see also
[
124
]
.

We take the self-consistent expansion as our starting point for our PM expansion in the far zone. Outside the source region, where
R_{\alpha\beta}[\mathring{g}+h]=0
, the self-consistent field equations (
104
) become

\displaystyle E_{\alpha\beta}[h^{[1]}]

\displaystyle=0,

(108a)

\displaystyle E_{\alpha\beta}[h^{[2]}]

\displaystyle=2\delta^{2}R_{\alpha\beta}[h^{[1]},h^{[1]}],

(108b)

where we have used
\delta R_{\alpha\beta}[h]=E_{\alpha\beta}[h]
in the Lorenz gauge. All information about the matter fields
\Psi
will now be transmitted to
h^{[n]}_{\alpha\beta}
through matching to the multiscale near-zone solution. We do not directly impose the gauge condition but instead allow it to be enforced indirectly through this matching; this suffices because the field equations automatically preserve the gauge condition if the boundary data satisfies it
[
166
]
.

The expansion (
101
) is, effectively, an expansion in powers of the mass
m_{2}
(while holding the orbital trajectory fixed). We next introduce a post-Minkowskian expansion by formally expanding in powers of the background mass
\mathring{m}_{1}
, under the assumption that in the weak-field region, the effect of
\mathring{m}_{1}
is a small, post-Minkowskian perturbation. Concretely, we expand at fixed Cartesian coordinates
(t,x^{i})
and at fixed
\Psi
. We define
x^{i}
from the tortoise polar coordinates
(r_{*},\theta,\phi)
, such that
r_{*}=\sqrt{\delta_{ij}x^{i}x^{j}}
. This ensures that the outgoing null surfaces of the Minkowski background
\eta_{\alpha\beta}
, defined by constant retarded time
u=t-r_{*}
, are also the outgoing null surfaces of
\mathring{g}_{\alpha\beta}
:
\eta^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u\,\partial_{\beta}u=0=\mathring{g}^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u\,\partial_{\beta}u=0
. The expansion puts the operators
E_{\alpha\beta}
and
\delta^{2}R_{\alpha\beta}
in the forms

\displaystyle E_{\alpha\beta}[h]

\displaystyle=\Box_{\eta}h_{\alpha\beta}+\sum_{j\geq 1}(\mathring{m}_{1})^{j}E^{[j]}_{\alpha\beta}[h],

(109)

\displaystyle\delta^{2}R_{\alpha\beta}[h,\ldots,h]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq 0}(\mathring{m}_{1})^{j}\delta^{2}R^{[j]}_{\alpha\beta}[h,\ldots,h],

(110)

and the metric perturbations in the form

h^{[n]}_{\alpha\beta}=\sum_{j\geq 0}(\mathring{m}_{1})^{j}h^{[n,j]}_{\alpha\beta}(t,x^{i};\Psi).

(111)

Here all components are in the coordinate basis
\partial_{\alpha}t
,
\partial_{\alpha}x^{i}
.

Equations (
109
) and (
110
) represent expansions in powers of
\mathring{m}_{1}/r
and
\mathring{a}/r
(
\propto\mathring{m_{1}}/r
) because they are expansions of operators constructed from the Kerr metric, which contains no length scale other than
\mathring{m}_{1}
. In contrast, equation (
111
) does not correspond simply to an expansion in powers of
\mathring{m}_{1}/r
; indeed, inverse length scales other than
1/r
, such as
\dot{\Omega}/\Omega
, arise to form dimensionless combinations with the dimensionful expansion parameter
\mathring{m}_{1}
. Also note that equation (
111
) is not an expansion in powers of
\mathring{m}_{1}\Omega
; we must avoid such an expansion because
\mathring{m}_{1}\Omega
is not small here (unlike in the PN-MPM context).

With these expansions, the field equations become a sequence of Minkowskian wave equations, one at each order in
m_{2}
and
\mathring{m}_{1}
. The equations read

\displaystyle\Box_{\eta}h^{[1,0]}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=0,

(112a)

\displaystyle\Box_{\eta}h^{[1,1]}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-E^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta}[h^{[1,0]}]\equiv S^{[1,1]}_{\alpha\beta},

(112b)

\displaystyle\hskip 6.5pt\vdots

\displaystyle\Box_{\eta}h^{[2,0]}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=2\delta^{2}R^{[0]}_{\alpha\beta}[h^{[1,0]},h^{[1,0]}]\equiv S^{[2,0]}_{\alpha\beta},

(112c)

\displaystyle\Box_{\eta}h^{[2,1]}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=2\delta^{2}R^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta}[h^{[1,0]},h^{[1,0]}]+4\delta^{2}R^{[0]}_{\alpha\beta}[h^{[1,0]},h^{[1,1]}]-E^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta}[h^{[2,0]}]\equiv S^{[2,1]}_{\alpha\beta},

(112d)

\displaystyle\hskip 6.5pt\vdots

We demand that the solutions to these equations match the multiscale solution when they are re-expanded in a common regime, meaning the multiscale solution is re-expanded for
r\gg M
and the far-zone solution is expanded for
r\ll M/\epsilon
. This shared regime is the ‘near far zone’ at
M\ll r\ll M/\epsilon
.

5.3
Algorithm for constructing the GSF-MPM far-zone solution

We now develop a scheme to solve the field equations (
112
) in a manner consistent (i) with retarded propagation and (ii) with matching to the multiscale near-zone solution. Since our method draws heavily on the PN-MPM formalism, we refer to it as a GSF-MPM scheme.

The retarded solutions take a form analogous to equations (135)–(136) in reference
[
137
]
. Explicitly, the retarded solution to the vacuum equation (
112a
), meaning the solution with no incoming radiation, can be written as
[
167
]

\displaystyle h^{[1,0]}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\geq 0}\partial_{L}\frac{F^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta L}(u)}{r_{*}},

(113)

where the multipole moments
F^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta L}
are STF in their last
l
indices, and

\partial_{i}r_{*}=n_{i}\equiv\delta_{ij}x^{j}/r_{*},

(114)

meaning
\partial_{i}u=-n_{i}
. As in the PN-MPM scheme, the multipole moments will encode the full freedom in our far-zone solution, and they are what will be determined by matching to the near-zone solution. Here it will be important that no corrections to the multipole moments appear in the higher-order terms
h^{[1,j]}_{\alpha\beta}
, such that
F^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta L}
on its own appropriately matches the coefficient of
1/r
appearing in the near-zone, multiscale solution. In other words, we require that no terms of order
1/r
appear in
h^{[1,j]}_{\alpha\beta}
for
j>0
.

Before proceeding to subleading orders
h^{[1,j]}_{\alpha\beta}
, we first explain the importance of avoiding new
1/r
terms in those subleading orders. Consider re-expanding (
113
) in multiscale form. For a function
K(u,\epsilon)
, the multiscale expansion reads

K(u,\epsilon)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\epsilon^{n}K_{(n)}^{m}(\Omega(u,\epsilon))e^{-im\phi_{p}(u,\epsilon)}.

(115)

Here for simplicity we assume that
\uptau=u
in the region where we perform the matching to the near zone, such that we do not need to expand
u
in terms of
\uptau
and
r_{*}
. Starting from a multiscale expansion of
F^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta L}
, we then have

\displaystyle h^{[1,0]}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-l}^{l}[im\Omega(u,\epsilon)]^{l}\hat{n}^{L}F^{[1],m}_{\alpha\beta L}(\Omega(u,\epsilon))e^{-im\phi_{p}(u,\epsilon)}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon,1/r^{2}),

(116)

observing that the leading term in
\epsilon
and
1/r
comes from
\partial_{L}
acting on the phase factor
e^{-im\phi_{p}}
in the multiscale expansion of
F^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta L}
. Recalling the notation for the STF projection
\hat{n}^{L}\equiv n^{\langle i_{1}}\cdots n^{i_{l}\rangle}
, we have used the fact the the multipolar moments are intrinsically STF in their indices to write
n^{L}F^{[1],m}_{\alpha\beta L}=\hat{n}^{L}F^{[1],m}_{\alpha\beta L}
. The contraction
\hat{n}^{L}F^{[1],m}_{\alpha\beta L}
is equal to an expansion of each Cartesian component in scalar (spin-weight 0) spherical harmonics,
\sum_{lm^{\prime}}F^{[1],mlm^{\prime}}_{\alpha\beta}(\Omega)Y_{lm^{\prime}}
. Matching to the near-zone solution will enforce
m^{\prime}=m
, such that

\displaystyle h^{[1,0]}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-l}^{l}(im\Omega)^{l}F^{[1],lm}_{\alpha\beta}(\Omega)Y_{lm}e^{-im\phi_{p}}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon,1/r^{2}),

(117)

discarding the now-extraneous second azithumal mode number
m^{\prime}
(i.e., defining
F^{[1],lm}_{\alpha\beta}\equiv F^{[1],mlm}_{\alpha\beta}
).

Now consider if we allowed additional
1/r
terms of the same form in each
h^{[1,j]}_{\alpha\beta}
, such that the total coefficient of
1/r
is proportional to the sum
F^{[1],lm}_{\alpha\beta}+\sum_{j>0}(\mathring{m}_{1})^{j}F^{[1,j],lm}_{\alpha\beta}
. This would need to match the
1/r
term in the large-
r
expansion of the multiscale solution
h^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
, say

h^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-l}^{l}G^{(1),lm}_{\alpha\beta}(\Omega)Y_{lm}e^{-im\phi_{p}}+\mathcal{O}(1/r^{2}),

(118)

which would imply
G^{(1),lm}_{\alpha\beta}=(im\Omega)^{l}\bigl[F^{[1],lm}_{\alpha\beta}+\sum_{j>0}(\mathring{m}_{1})^{j}F^{[1,j],lm}_{\alpha\beta}\bigr]
. Such an equality would not be useful because
G^{(1),lm}_{\alpha\beta}
is obtained numerically on a grid of
\Omega
values; there is not a sense in which we can further expand it in powers of
\mathring{m}_{1}
in order to match each term
F^{[1,j],lm}_{\alpha\beta}
. More fundamentally, the corrections
F^{[1,j],lm}_{\alpha\beta}
are ruled out by dimensional analysis.
F^{[1,j],lm}_{\alpha\beta}
is a function of
\Omega
only, and each term
(\mathring{m}_{1})^{j}F^{[1,j],lm}_{\alpha\beta}
must have the same dimension as
F^{[1],lm}_{\alpha\beta}
. Therefore the only possible form these higher-order terms can take is
(\mathring{m}_{1}\Omega)^{j}b^{[1,j],lm}_{\alpha\beta}
for some dimensionless numbers
b^{[1,j],lm}_{\alpha\beta}
. Such an expansion is only sensible if
\mathring{m}_{1}\Omega\ll 1
— the slow-velocity, PN limit. Hence, we must exclude such higher-order terms to keep our GSF-MPM expansion relativistic.

To construct the subleading terms without introducing these problematic new
1/r
outgoing waves, we adopt the solution

h^{[1,j]}_{\alpha\beta}=\mathop{\mathrm{FP}}_{B=0}\Box^{-1}_{\eta}\left(r^{B}_{*}S^{[1,j]}_{\alpha\beta}\right)+k^{[1,j]}_{\alpha\beta}.

(119)

Here
\Box^{-1}_{\eta}f
denotes the integral
\int G(t-t^{\prime},\vec{x},\vec{x}^{\prime})f(t^{\prime},\vec{x}^{\prime})dt^{\prime}d^{3}x^{\prime}
against the flat-spacetime retarded Green’s function,

G(t-t^{\prime},\vec{x},\vec{x}^{\prime})=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{\delta(t-t^{\prime}-|\vec{x}-\vec{x}^{\prime}|)}{|\vec{x}-\vec{x}^{\prime}|}.

(120)

We have introduced a Hadamard finite part operation
\mathop{\mathrm{FP}}_{B=0}
because
S^{[1,j]}_{\alpha\beta}
diverges at
r_{*}=0
, as is clear from the form (
113
) (see
[
106
]
for the origin of this finite part regularization procedure in the PN-MPM context). However, note that
\mathop{\mathrm{FP}}_{B=0}\Box^{-1}_{\eta}\left(r^{B}_{*}S^{[1,j]}_{\alpha\beta}\right)
is a particular solution to
\Box_{\eta}h^{[1,j]}_{\alpha\beta}=S^{[i,j]}_{\alpha\beta}
, with causal propagation, for all
r_{*}>0
. The second term in equation (
119
),
k^{[1,j]}_{\alpha\beta}
, is a homogeneous solution of the form (
113
), such that equation (
119
) represents the most general causal solution to
\Box_{\eta}h^{[1,j]}_{\alpha\beta}=S^{[i,j]}_{\alpha\beta}
. We choose
k^{[1,j]}_{\alpha\beta}
through our requirement that
h^{[1,j]}_{\alpha\beta}
contains no terms of the form
G_{\alpha\beta}(u,n^{i})/r_{*}
. The integral
\mathop{\mathrm{FP}}_{B=0}\Box^{-1}_{\eta}(r^{B}_{*}S^{[1,j]}_{\alpha\beta})
generically involves outgoing waves of that form, which we precisely cancel with
k^{[1,j]}_{\alpha\beta}
. Note that this involves no loss of generality: the solution
\sum_{j\geq 0}(\mathring{m}_{1})^{j}h^{[1,j]}_{\alpha\beta}
is the most general solution to equation (
108a
) containing no incoming radiation; all the freedom in that solution is contained in the multipole moments
F^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta L}(u)
in
h^{[1,0]}_{\alpha\beta}
.

We follow the same general procedure in solving the higher-order field equations in the sequence (
112
). The most general solution to equation (
112c
) with no incoming radiation can be written as

h^{[n,j]}_{\alpha\beta}=\mathop{\mathrm{FP}}_{B=0}\Box^{-1}_{\eta}\left(r^{B}_{*}S^{[n,j]}_{\alpha\beta}\right)+k^{[n,j]}_{\alpha\beta}.

(121)

Here
k^{[n,j]}_{\alpha\beta}
is a homogeneous solution of the form (
113
). For each
n
, the leading homogeneous solution,
k^{[n,0]}_{\alpha\beta}
, is left arbitrary, to be determined through matching to the near-zone solution. The subleading homogeneous solutions,
k^{[n,j]}_{\alpha\beta}
with
j>0
, are chosen to cancel any outgoing radiation in
\mathop{\mathrm{FP}}_{B=0}\Box^{-1}_{\eta}\left(r^{B}_{*}S^{[n,j]}_{\alpha\beta}\right)
. This contrasts with the traditional MPM scheme, where
k^{[n,j]}_{\alpha\beta}
would be chosen to enforce the gauge condition on each
h^{[n,j]}_{\alpha\beta}
; we avoid that choice in order to isolate the outgoing wave entirely in the leading PM term
h^{[n,0]}_{\alpha\beta}
at each order in
\epsilon
, recalling that the gauge condition will be enforced through matching to the near-zone solution.

As illustrated in section
5.1
, the breakdown of the multiscale expansion at second order in the near zone is specifically associated with the presence of
1/r^{2}
source terms.
9
9

9

A practical consequence of our choice of tortoise coordinates
x^{i}
is that no source terms
\sim 1/r_{*}^{2}
arise in the field equations for
h^{[1,j]}_{\alpha\beta}
.
This breakdown is cured, and physical boundary conditions are found for our near-zone multiscale field
h^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}
, by examining the part of
h^{[2,0]}_{\alpha\beta}
sourced by the most slowly falling piece of
S^{[2,0]}_{\alpha\beta}
. Writing

S^{[2,0]}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{s_{\alpha\beta}(u,n^{i})}{r^{2}_{*}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{1}{r_{*}^{3}}\right),

(122)

we define

j_{\alpha\beta}=\mathop{\mathrm{FP}}_{B=0}\Box^{-1}_{\eta}\left(r^{B-2}_{*}s_{\alpha\beta}\right).

(123)

The retarded integral converges even without the finite part operation, since
1/r^{2}_{*}
is integrable at
r_{*}=0
, meaning we can equivalently write

j_{\alpha\beta}=\Box^{-1}_{\eta}\left(r_{*}^{-2}s_{\alpha\beta}\right).

(124)

We then define

\Delta j_{\alpha\beta}\equiv\mathop{\mathrm{FP}}_{B=0}\Box^{-1}_{\eta}\left(r^{B}_{*}S^{[2,0]}_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{s_{\alpha\beta}(u,n^{i})}{r^{2}_{*}}\right)=\mathop{\mathrm{FP}}_{B=0}\Box^{-1}_{\eta}\left(r^{B}_{*}S^{[2,0]}_{\alpha\beta}\right)-j_{\alpha\beta}.

(125)

This can be decomposed into an outgoing-wave homogeneous solution plus a particular solution to
\Box_{\eta}\Delta j_{\alpha\beta}=(S^{[2,0]}_{\alpha\beta}-r_{*}^{-2}s_{\alpha\beta})
that decays more rapidly than
1/r
toward infinity. The particular solution is constructed in the same manner as described previously: calculate the (finite part) retarded integral, read off the large-
r
coefficient of
1/r
in the solution, and then subtract an outgoing-wave homogeneous solution with that same coefficient of
1/r
. The homogeneous solution, of the form (
113
), we denote
\Delta k^{[2,0]}_{\alpha\beta}
. This allows us to write
h^{[2,0]}_{\alpha\beta}
in the alternative form

h^{[2,0]}_{\alpha\beta}=j_{\alpha\beta}+\tilde{k}^{[2,0]}_{\alpha\beta}+\left(\Delta j_{\alpha\beta}-\Delta k^{[2,0]}_{\alpha\beta}\right).

(126)

The second term,
\tilde{k}^{[2,0]}_{\alpha\beta}\equiv k^{[2,0]}_{\alpha\beta}+\Delta k^{[2,0]}_{\alpha\beta}
, is a homogeneous, outgoing-wave solution, while the combination of terms in parentheses decays more rapidly than
1/r
toward future null infinity, meaning it will not contribute to the emitted GWs.

In the next sections we show the following:

(i)

j_{\alpha\beta}
is sourced by the emitted GW flux (plus oscillatory source terms).

(ii)

When re-expanded in the near far zone
M\ll r\ll M/\epsilon
,
j_{\alpha\beta}
provides a boundary condition for
h^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}
that introduces nonlocal-in-time terms into the orbital dynamics. We define a certain piece of this re-expansion of
j_{\alpha\beta}
as a puncture field
h^{(2)\mathcal{}P}_{\alpha\beta}
(so called because it is singular at the large-
r
limit of the near zone), and we adopt the residual field
h^{(2)\mathcal{}R}_{\alpha\beta}=h^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}-h^{(2)\mathcal{}P}_{\alpha\beta}
as the numerical variable in the near zone
[
139
]
.

(iii)

When re-expanded in the very far zone
r\gg M/\epsilon
, the quasistationary piece of
j_{\alpha\beta}
becomes the first-order GW memory
h^{(1)}_{l0}
in equation (
60
).

(iv)

The numerically calculated
h^{(2)\mathcal{}R}_{\alpha\beta}
determines the nonhereditary oscillatory part of the second-order waveform
h^{(2)}_{lm}
in equation (
56
).

(v)

Oscillatory modes of
h^{[3]}_{\alpha\beta}
can be sourced by products of first-order memory modes and first-order oscillatory modes, promoting them by
1/\epsilon
. These oscillatory terms might contribute to the oscillatory modes of
h^{(2)}_{lm}
in equation (
56
), and consequently they could contribute to the second-order GW memory
h^{(2)}_{l0}
.

5.4
Asymptotic source and GW flux

The source for
j_{\alpha\beta}
is the
1/r^{2}_{*}
term in
2\delta^{2}R_{\alpha\beta}
, which is computable entirely from the
1/r_{*}
term in
h^{[1,0]}_{\alpha\beta}
. We write that term compactly as

h^{[1,0]}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{f^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta}(u,n^{i})}{r_{*}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}_{*}}\right).

(127)

In this section we develop a useful decomposition of
f^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta}
into a mass piece plus oscillatory pieces. We then derive an expression for the coefficient of
1/r^{2}_{*}
in
2\delta^{2}R_{\alpha\beta}
in terms of those pieces, which will be the starting point for our subsequent derivations.

Equation (
127
) must match the
1/r
term in the multiscale solution
h^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
. We see from equations (
117
) and (
118
) that this term can be divided into an oscillatory piece (
m\neq 0
) and a quasistationary monopole (
l=0
). More explicitly, we can write

h^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{2M_{\mathrm{B}}^{(1)}(u)\delta_{\alpha\beta}+z^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}(u,n^{i})}{r_{*}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{\ln r}{r_{*}^{2}}\right)

(128)

in
(t,x^{i})
coordinates. Here
z^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
is an oscillatory function made up of
m\neq 0
modes, and
M^{(1)}_{\mathrm{B}}
represents a contribution to the Bondi mass coming from the particle’s leading-order orbital energy
m_{2}{\mathcal{}E}_{(0)}
as well as the evolving correction
\delta m_{1}
to the primary black hole’s mass
[
138
]
:

\epsilon M^{(1)}_{\mathrm{B}}=\epsilon\delta m_{1}+m_{2}{\mathcal{}E}_{(0)}.

(129)

The Kronecker-delta form of the mass term in equation (
128
) follows from the Lorenz gauge condition.

Given equations (
127
) and (
128
), matching between the near- and far-zone solutions requires

f^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta}=z^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}(u,n^{a})+2M^{(1)}_{\mathrm{B}}(u)\,\delta_{\alpha\beta}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon).

(130)

The order-
\epsilon
differences arise because the matching condition involves a re-expansion of
h^{[1,0]}_{\alpha\beta}
. In the remainder of our calculations, we will work with the leading-order approximation, discarding the
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
remainder.

To evaluate the source for
j_{\alpha\beta}
, we further specify
z^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
using the Lorenz gauge condition (
102
). The gauge condition simplifies due to

\mathring{\nabla}_{\gamma}\frac{f^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta}(u,n^{i})}{r_{*}}=-\frac{\dot{f}^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta}k_{\gamma}}{r_{*}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}_{*}}\right),

(131)

where a dot indicates differentiation with respect to
u
,

k_{\alpha}\equiv-\partial_{\alpha}u=(-1,n_{i})

(132)

is the outgoing principal null vector, and there is no contribution from a derivative acting on the
n^{i}
dependence in
f^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta}
because
\partial_{i}n^{j}\propto 1/r_{*}
. It immediately follows that the gauge condition imposes

\dot{z}^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}k^{\beta}=\frac{1}{2}\dot{z}^{(1)}k_{\alpha},

(133)

with
z^{(1)}\equiv\eta^{\alpha\beta}z^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
. Integrating this with the initial condition
z^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}(-\infty)=0
gives us

z^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}k^{\beta}=\frac{1}{2}z^{(1)}k_{\alpha}.

(134)

In retarded polar coordinates
(u,r_{*},\theta^{A})
, we have
k^{\alpha}=(0,1,0,0)
and
k_{\alpha}=(-1,0,0,0)
. Equation (
134
) then implies

z^{(1)}_{r_{*}r_{*}}=z^{(1)}_{r_{*}A}=\Omega^{AB}z^{(1)}_{AB}=0.

(135)

The last equality shows that
z^{(1)}_{AB}
is equal (up to a factor of
r_{*}^{2}
) to the Bondi shear contained in
h^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta}
:

z^{(1)}_{AB}=z^{(1)}_{\langle AB\rangle}=r_{*}^{2}C^{[1]}_{AB}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon),

(136)

where we recall that the angular brackets denote the STF combination of indices with respect to the unit-sphere metric
\Omega_{AB}
. Note that
C^{[1]}_{AB}
accounts for the full oscillatory part of the shear at linear order in the mass ratio, but it does not include the first-order memory contribution, which will emerge from a
1/\epsilon
promotion of
j_{\alpha\beta}
. We reserve the symbol
C^{(1)}_{AB}
for the complete linear-in-
\epsilon
contribution to the waveform (
56
).
Now substituting equation (
130
) into the explicit expression for
2\delta^{2}R_{\alpha\beta}
— equation (6) of
[
77
]
— using equation (
134
), and simplifying, we find

s_{\alpha\beta}=-\Pi\,k_{\alpha}k_{\beta}+\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\bigl(\bar{z}^{\mu\nu}_{(1)}\dot{z}^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}\bigr)k_{\alpha}k_{\beta}+4M^{(1)}_{\mathrm{B}}\ddot{z}^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}q^{\mu}_{\alpha}q^{\nu}_{\beta},

(137)

which should be compared to (2.10) of
[
9
]
in the PN-MPM context.
Here,
q^{\mu}_{\alpha}\equiv\delta^{\mu}_{\alpha}+n^{\mu}k_{\alpha}
projects orthogonally to the ingoing principal null vector
n^{\mu}
, whose Cartesian components are
n^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}(1,-n^{i})
, and we have defined the quantity

\Pi\equiv\frac{1}{2}\dot{z}^{\alpha\beta}_{(1)}\dot{z}^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{4}\dot{z}^{(1)}\dot{z}^{(1)}.

(138)

Using the coordinate form (
135
) of the gauge condition, it is straightforward to relate
\Pi
to the angular distribution of GW flux (
12
) carried by
h^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
:

\Pi=\frac{1}{2}\dot{C}^{AB}_{[1]}\dot{C}_{AB}^{[1]}=16\pi\left.\frac{d^{2}E^{\mathrm{G}W}}{dud\Omega_{2}}\right|_{h^{[1]}}.

(139)

Equation (
135
) also allows us to write the second and third terms in equation (
137
) in terms of the Bondi shear and Bondi news, leading to another useful form of equation (
137
),

s_{\alpha\beta}=\left[-\frac{1}{2}\dot{C}^{AB}_{[1]}\dot{C}_{AB}^{[1]}+\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\left(C^{AB}_{[1]}\dot{C}^{[1]}_{AB}\right)\right]k_{\alpha}k_{\beta}+4r_{*}^{2}M^{[1]}_{\mathrm{B}}\ddot{C}^{[1]}_{AB}\Omega^{A}_{\alpha}\Omega^{B}_{\beta},

(140)

where
\Omega^{A}_{\alpha}\equiv r_{*}^{-2}\Omega^{AB}\eta_{\alpha\beta}({\partial x^{\beta}}/{\partial\theta^{B}})
reduces to
\delta^{A}_{\alpha}
in retarded polar coordinates
(u,r_{*},\theta^{A})
and to
{r_{*}^{-1}}\Omega^{AB}\delta_{\alpha i}({\partial n^{i}}/{\partial\theta^{B}})
in Cartesian coordinates
(t,x^{i})
.
Note that the second and third terms in equation (
137
) vanish if averaged over
\phi_{p}
(up to terms that are higher order in
\epsilon
, which arise from the
u
derivative in the second term acting on the
\Omega
dependence of
z_{\alpha\beta}
). Equations (
137
) and (
140
) hence split
s_{\alpha\beta}
into a flux term plus a purely oscillatory one,

\delta^{2}R_{\alpha\beta}[h^{[1]},h^{[1]}]=\frac{8\pi k_{\alpha}k_{\beta}}{r^{2}}\left.\frac{d^{2}E^{\mathrm{G}W}}{dud\Omega_{2}}\right|_{h^{[1]}}+\text{oscillatory terms}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{1}{r^{3}}\right).

(141)

Moreover, this same equality holds for
\delta^{2}G_{\alpha\beta}
since all three terms in equation (
137
) have vanishing trace. The fact that
\delta^{2}G_{\alpha\beta}[h^{[1]},h^{[1]}]=\frac{8\pi k_{\alpha}k_{\beta}}{r^{2}}\left.\frac{d^{2}E^{\mathrm{G}W}}{du\,d\Omega_{2}}\right|_{h^{[1]}}
on average is the familiar statement that the averaged
\delta^{2}G_{\alpha\beta}/(8\pi)
can be thought of as an effective stress-energy tensor of the emitted GWs
[
168
]
. The oscillatory terms, in particular the final term in equation (
140
), give rise to GW tails
[
9
]
.

5.5
Puncture fields and memory in the near zone
r\ll M/\epsilon

To obtain physical boundary conditions for the near-zone solution, we must re-expand our far-zone solution
\sum_{n,j}\epsilon^{n}(\mathring{m}_{1})^{j}h^{[n,j]}_{\alpha\beta}
for
r\ll M/\epsilon
— or equivalently, for
\epsilon\ll M/r
.

Here we focus on the leading second-order term,
h^{[2,0]}_{\alpha\beta}
, which suffices to resolve the infrared divergence in the near zone. More specifically, we focus on the dominant piece of
h^{[2,0]}_{\alpha\beta}
, namely
j_{\alpha\beta}=\Box^{-1}_{\eta}\left[r_{*}^{-2}s_{\alpha\beta}(u,n^{i})\right]
, which contributes the dominant large-
r
behavior in the near zone and resolves the infrared divergence.

We first note that the function
s_{\alpha\beta}(u,n^{i})
, as given in equation (
137
), is constructed from the coefficient of
1/r_{*}
in
h^{[1,0]}_{\alpha\beta}
, meaning it can be calculated from the multipole moments
F^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta L}(u)
in equation (
113
). As explained below that equation, the multipole moments inherit a multiscale form through matching to the coefficient of
1/r
in the near-zone solution
h^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
. Hence, we can write

s_{\alpha\beta}(u,n^{i})=\sum_{l\geq 0}s_{\alpha\beta L}(u)\hat{n}^{L}=\sum_{l\geq 0}\sum_{m=-l}^{l}s^{m}_{\alpha\beta L}(\Omega(u,\epsilon))e^{-im\phi_{p}(u,\epsilon)}\hat{n}^{L}

(142)

up to
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
corrections.
j_{\alpha\beta}
can then be written as

j_{\alpha\beta}=\Box^{-1}_{\eta}\left(r_{*}^{-2}s_{\alpha\beta}\right)=\sum_{l\geq 0}\sum_{m=-l}^{l}\Box^{-1}_{\eta}\left[r_{*}^{-2}s^{m}_{\alpha\beta L}(\Omega(u,\epsilon))e^{-im\phi_{p}(u,\epsilon)}\hat{n}^{L}\right],

(143)

again up to
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
corrections.
Reference
[
137
]
derived near-zone expansions of such integrals, exploiting methods from reference
[
80
]
. Here we will merely quote the results and discuss the implications.

Since
{\hat{n}}^{L}
diagonalizes
\Box^{-1}_{\eta}
,
j_{\alpha\beta}
naturally decomposes into the
{\hat{n}}^{L}
basis,

j_{\alpha\beta}=\sum_{l\geq 0}j_{\alpha\beta L}(u,r_{*},\epsilon){\hat{n}}^{L},

(144)

with
j_{\alpha\beta L}{\hat{n}}^{L}=\Box^{-1}_{\eta}\left[r_{*}^{-2}s_{\alpha\beta L}\hat{n}^{L}\right]
. Moreover, as shown in reference
[
137
]
,
\Box^{-1}_{\eta}
also preserves the expansion in Fourier modes
e^{-im\phi_{p}}
, such that

j_{\alpha\beta}=\sum_{m=-\infty}^{\infty}j^{m}_{\alpha\beta}(\Omega(u,\epsilon),r_{*},n^{i})e^{-im\phi_{p}(u,\epsilon)}=\sum_{l\geq 0}\sum_{m=-l}^{l}j^{m}_{\alpha\beta L}(\Omega(u,\epsilon),r_{*}){\hat{n}}^{L}e^{-im\phi_{p}(u,\epsilon)}.

(145)

with
j^{m}_{\alpha\beta L}{\hat{n}}^{L}e^{-im\phi_{p}}=\Box^{-1}_{\eta}[s^{m}_{\alpha\beta L}{\hat{n}}^{L}e^{-im\phi_{p}}]+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
.

For oscillatory,
m\neq 0
modes, reference
[
137
]
found that the harmonic coefficients in the near-zone expansion of equation (
143
) are given by

j^{m}_{\alpha\beta L}=\frac{\ln r}{2im\Omega r}s^{m}_{\alpha\beta L}(\Omega(u))+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{1}{r},\epsilon\right),

(146)

where we emphasise the
\mathcal{O}(1/r)
terms contain no
\ln r
. The choice of length scale in the logarithm is discussed in
Appendix A
. For simplicity we suppress the
\epsilon
dependence of
\Omega
here and below. Equation (
146
) agrees with the solution (
99
) found directly from the near-zone retarded integral, after converting (
99
) to
\uptau
-slicing by removing the exponential. In other words, matching to a far-zone solution is not strictly needed for
m\neq 0
modes at first and second order in
\epsilon
; the correct solution is obtained in the near zone without any additional information from the far zone. However, because of its irregular,
\ln(r)/r
behavior, treating
j^{m}_{\alpha\beta}
as a puncture is still most practical, subtracting it from the numerical variable and solving for the residual.

For quasistationary,
m=0
modes, reference
[
137
]
derived the following near-zone expansion of equation (
143
):

\displaystyle\left\langle{j}_{\alpha\beta L}\right\rangle=\begin{cases}\left\langle s_{\alpha\beta L}(\Omega)\right\rangle[\ln(2r)-1]-\int^{u}_{-\infty}\!\!dz\,\,\frac{d\left\langle s_{\alpha\beta L}(\Omega(z))\right\rangle}{dz}\ln(u-z)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)&if $l=0$,\\
-\displaystyle\frac{\left\langle s_{\alpha\beta L}(\Omega)\right\rangle}{l(l+1)}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)&if $l>0$,\end{cases}

(147)

where we adopt the
\left\langle\right\rangle
notation for
m=0
modes, as in equation (
88
). This result can be compared to equation (5.22) of
[
80
]
in the PN-MPM case. Note that in this instance any choice of length scale in the logarithms cancels between the terms inside and outside the integral.

Unlike in the oscillatory case, in equation (
147
) there are no omitted terms of order
\epsilon^{0}/r
; omitted terms all come with powers of
\epsilon
and higher, rather than lower, powers of
r
. However, the next order in the GSF-MPM expansion,
h^{[2,1]}_{\alpha\beta}
, involves terms of order
\ln(r_{*})/r_{*}
sourced by
E^{[1]}_{\alpha\beta}[j]
. These terms must be included in
h^{(2)\mathcal{}P}_{\alpha\beta}
in order to ensure the
1/r
terms in
h^{(2)\mathcal{}R}_{\alpha\beta}
represent a regular, outgoing homogeneous solution. However, we leave the description of
h^{[2,1]}_{\alpha\beta}
to future work as it does not directly enter into the calculation of GW memory at the orders in
\epsilon
we consider.

The most important aspect of
\left\langle{j}_{\alpha\beta L}\right\rangle
is the presence of a hereditary integral over all past times. Such nonlocal-in-time behavior could not be predicted within the multiscale expansion. This term will enter the multiscale solution
\left\langle h^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}\right\rangle=h^{(2),0}_{\alpha\beta}
and influence the conservative sector of the 2SF dynamics, in analogy with memory effects that enter the 5PN dynamics
[
85
,
86
]
.

At this order in
\epsilon
the memory contribution is confined to
l=0
due to a suppression by
\epsilon^{l}
that arises when re-expanding
j_{\alpha\beta}
in the near zone
[
137
]
. However, the reader should recall that
l
here refers to an expansion of Cartesian components in scalar spherical harmonics. One can straightforwardly check, using the tensorial structure
\left\langle{j}_{\alpha\beta L}\right\rangle\propto k_{\alpha}k_{\beta}
, that the hereditary integral contributes to both
l=0
and
l=2
(
m
=0) terms in an expansion in spin-weighted or tensor harmonics. To see this, we recall that the
m=0
piece of
s_{\alpha\beta}
is given by
-\left\langle\Pi\right\rangle k_{\alpha}k_{\beta}
, where
\Pi
is related to the GW flux by equation (
139
). Expanding
\Pi
as

\Pi=\sum_{l\geq 0}\Pi_{L}{\hat{n}}^{L}

(148)

and using equations (2.7) and (2.13) of reference
[
167
]
, we find

\displaystyle\left\langle s_{tt}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\sum_{l\geq 0}\left\langle\Pi_{L}\right\rangle{\hat{n}}^{L},

(149a)

\displaystyle\left\langle s_{ta}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\geq 0}\left({\hat{n}}_{aL}\left\langle\Pi_{L}\right\rangle+\frac{l}{2l+1}{\hat{n}}_{L-1}\left\langle\Pi_{aL-1}\right\rangle\right),

(149b)

\displaystyle\left\langle s_{ab}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\sum_{l\geq 0}\biggl({\hat{n}}_{abL}\left\langle\Pi_{L}\right\rangle+\frac{2l}{2l+3}{\hat{n}}_{L-1(a}\left\langle\Pi_{b)L-1}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{2l+3}\delta_{ab}{\hat{n}}_{L}\left\langle\Pi_{L}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 25.0pt\hskip 25.0pt+\frac{l(l-1)}{(2l+1)(2l-1)}{\hat{n}}_{L-2}\left\langle\Pi_{abL-2}\right\rangle\biggr).

(149c)
