and equation (2.7) of
[
167
]
to decompose
n^{i}{\hat{n}}^{L}
into STF tensors. We then find, in
(t,x^{i})
coordinates,

\displaystyle\lambda^{t}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2r}\sum_{l\geq 0}{\hat{n}}_{L}k_{l}\!\left[\int^{u}_{-\infty}\!\left\langle\Pi_{L}(u^{\prime})\right\rangle du^{\prime}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\ln r}{r^{2}}\right),

(A.22a)

\displaystyle\lambda^{a}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2r}\sum_{l\geq 0}\Biggl\{{\hat{n}}_{aL}k_{l+1}\!\left[\int^{u}_{-\infty}\!\left\langle\Pi_{L}(u^{\prime})\right\rangle du^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 25.0pt+\frac{l}{2l+1}{\hat{n}}_{L-1}k_{l-1}\!\left[\int^{u}_{-\infty}\!\left\langle\Pi_{aL-1}(u^{\prime})\right\rangle du^{\prime}\right]\Biggr\}+\left(\frac{\ln r}{r^{2}}\right),

(A.22b)

where for convenience we have introduced

k_{l}[f_{L}(u)]\equiv\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!dz\,\left(\frac{1}{2}\ln\frac{z}{2r_{*}}+H_{l}\right)f_{L}(u-z).

(A.23)

We see that
\lambda^{\alpha}
is of order
1/\epsilon^{2}
due to the double integration over
u
, such that
\epsilon^{2}\lambda^{\alpha}=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})
. However, because
\epsilon^{2}\lambda^{\alpha}
is of order
\ln(r)/r
, it can still be treated as a perturbative transformation. Moreover, it can only contribute at order
1/r
when a
u
derivative acts on it, demoting its impact on the metric to
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon/r)
. Since we limit our analysis to
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
terms for the quasistationary part of the metric, we only require
{\mathcal{}L}_{\lambda}\eta_{\alpha\beta}=2\partial_{(\alpha}\lambda_{\beta)}
in the transformation law (
A.2
); all other terms are either
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
or
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon r^{-2}\ln r)
.

Calculating
\left\langle j_{\alpha\beta}\right\rangle^{\prime}=\left\langle j_{\alpha\beta}\right\rangle+2\partial_{(\alpha}\lambda_{\beta)}
involves the following steps: (i) combining equations (
157
) and (
149
) to calculate
\left\langle j_{\alpha\beta}\right\rangle
; (ii) noting that
1/r
terms in
\partial_{(\alpha}\lambda_{\beta)}
only arise when derivatives act on
u
, and that these derivatives pass through
k_{l}
to collapse the integrals over
u
; (iii) using
\partial_{\alpha}u=-k_{\alpha}=(-1,n_{a})
and equation (2.7) of
[
167
]
to decompose
\partial_{(\alpha}\lambda_{\beta)}
into STF tensors
{\hat{n}}_{L}
; (iv) summing
\left\langle j_{\alpha\beta}\right\rangle+2\partial_{(\alpha}\lambda_{\beta)}
and writing all
{\hat{n}}_{L}
combinations in terms of
n_{L}
using equations (2.7), (2.13), and (2.14) of
[
167
]
. The result of these straightforward but lengthy manipulations is

\displaystyle\left\langle j_{tt}\right\rangle^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(r^{-2}\ln r),

(A.24a)

\displaystyle\left\langle j_{ta}\right\rangle^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2r}\sum_{l\geq 0}\frac{1}{l+1}\int_{-\infty}^{u}du^{\prime}\left\langle n_{aL}\Pi_{L}(u^{\prime})-n_{L-1}\Pi_{aL-1}(u^{\prime})\right\rangle+\mathcal{O}(r^{-2}\ln r),

(A.24b)

\displaystyle\left\langle j_{ab}\right\rangle^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{r}\sum_{l\geq 0}\frac{1}{(l+1)(l+2)}\int_{-\infty}^{u}du^{\prime}\Bigl\langle(l+1)n_{abL}\Pi_{L}(u^{\prime})-(l-2)n_{L-1(a}\Pi_{b)L-1}(u^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hskip 25.0pt\hskip 25.0pt-\delta_{ab}n_{L}\Pi_{L}(u^{\prime})-2n_{L-2}\Pi_{abL-2}(u^{\prime})\Bigr\rangle+\mathcal{O}(r^{-2}\ln r).

(A.24c)

Converted to
(u,x^{a})
coordinates, the components are

\displaystyle\left\langle j_{uu}\right\rangle^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\langle j_{tt}\right\rangle^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}(r^{-2}\ln r),

(A.25a)

\displaystyle\left\langle j_{ua}\right\rangle^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\langle j_{ta}\right\rangle^{\prime}+\left\langle j_{tt}\right\rangle^{\prime}n_{a}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2r}\sum_{l\geq 1}\frac{n_{L-1}}{l+1}\perp_{ai}\int_{-\infty}^{u}du^{\prime}\left\langle\Pi_{iL-1}(u^{\prime})\right\rangle+\mathcal{O}(r^{-2}\ln r),

(A.25b)

\displaystyle\left\langle j_{ab}\right\rangle^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\langle j_{ab}\right\rangle^{\prime}+2\left\langle j_{t(a}\right\rangle^{\prime}n_{b)}+\left\langle j_{tt}\right\rangle^{\prime}n_{a}n_{b}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{l\geq 2}\frac{2n_{L-2}}{(l+1)(l+2)}\perp^{\mathrm{T}T}_{ab,ij}\int_{-\infty}^{u}du^{\prime}\left\langle\Pi_{ijL-2}(u^{\prime})\right\rangle+\mathcal{O}(r^{-2}\ln r),

(A.25c)

where we note that
j_{ab}
on the left-hand side denotes components in
(u,x^{a})
coordinates, while
j_{ab}
on the right-hand side denotes components in
(t,x^{a})
coordinates. Here
\perp_{ai}
and
\perp^{\mathrm{T}T}_{ab,ij}
are the projectors defined in equation (
2
); these projectors are not inserted by hand but arise from the transformation to retarded coordinates.

It is now trivial to check that
\left\langle j_{\alpha\beta}\right\rangle^{\prime}
in equation (
A.25
) satisfies the Bondi-Sachs gauge conditions (
A.1
). Translated to Cartesian coordinates
(u,x^{a})
, these conditions read

\left\langle j_{ab}\right\rangle^{\prime}n^{b}=0=\left\langle j_{ab}\right\rangle^{\prime}\delta^{ab}.

(A.26)

We immediately see these are satisfied due to the TT projector
\perp^{\mathrm{T}T}_{ab,ij}
in equation (
A.25
). Explicitly, the nonzero polar components are

\left\langle j_{uA}\right\rangle^{\prime}=\Omega^{a}_{A}\left\langle j_{ua}\right\rangle^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad\left\langle j_{AB}\right\rangle^{\prime}=\Omega_{A}^{a}\Omega^{b}_{B}\left\langle j_{ab}\right\rangle^{\prime},

(A.27)

where
\Omega^{a}_{A}\equiv\frac{\partial x^{a}}{\partial\theta^{A}}=r\frac{\partial n^{a}}{\partial\theta^{A}}
automatically projects out components along
n^{a}
, such that

\Omega^{a}_{A}\perp_{ai}\,=\Omega^{i}_{A}\quad\text{and}\quad\Omega^{a}_{A}\Omega^{b}_{B}\perp^{\mathrm{T}T}_{ab,ij}\,=\Omega^{(i}_{A}\Omega^{j)}_{B}-\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{AB}\delta^{ij}.

(A.28)

Appendix A.5
Summary: waveform and phase redefinition

Having completed our transformation, we now relabel our final, Bondi-Sachs coordinates as
X^{\alpha}=(U,R,\Theta^{A})
. They are related to the original coordinates
(u,r,\theta^{A})
by

X^{\alpha}=x^{\alpha}-\epsilon\xi^{\alpha}-\epsilon^{2}\left(\zeta^{\alpha}+\lambda^{\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}\xi^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}\xi^{\alpha}\right)+\mathcal{O}(3).

(A.29)

Our total metric in these coordinates is obtained by combining equations (
A.20
) and (
A.25
). It reads

g^{\mathrm{B}S}_{\alpha\beta}=\eta_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{2M_{\mathrm{B}}(U)}{R}k_{\alpha}k_{\beta}+\frac{\left\langle c_{\alpha\beta}(U,\Theta^{A})\right\rangle}{R}+\frac{z_{\alpha\beta}(U,\Theta^{A})}{R}+\ldots

(A.30)

Here we have defined
z_{\alpha\beta}\equiv\epsilon z^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}+\epsilon^{2}z^{(2)\mathcal{}R}_{\alpha\beta}
as the total oscillatory coefficient of
1/R
. Similarly, we have defined
\left\langle c_{\alpha\beta}(U,\Theta^{A})\right\rangle
as the total non-oscillatory,
l>0
coefficient of
1/R
:

\left\langle c_{\alpha\beta}\right\rangle=\epsilon\left\langle c^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}\right\rangle+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})=\epsilon^{2}R\left\langle j_{\alpha\beta}\right\rangle^{\prime}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2},1/R),

(A.31)

noting that
\left\langle j_{\alpha\beta}\right\rangle^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}(1/\epsilon)
due to the memory-type integrals in equation (
A.25
). This term’s contribution to the shear is
\left\langle C_{AB}\right\rangle=R^{-2}\left\langle c_{AB}\right\rangle
.

Equation (
A.30
) confirms that the near-zone
1/r
coefficients
z^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}
and
z^{(2)\mathcal{}R}_{\alpha\beta}
directly yield the asymptotic oscillatory part of the waveform, as promised in section
5.6
, up to possible
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
memory distortion terms that arise from the omitted
\mathcal{O}(3)
pieces of the metric.

We can likewise now confirm that
\left\langle j_{ab}\right\rangle^{\prime}
represents the first-order GW memory. Equation (
A.25
) is precisely the linear-in-mass-ratio part of the GW memory as written in equation (224) of reference
[
211
]
. (The sign difference arises from use of the field variable
h^{\alpha\beta}=\sqrt{-g}g^{\alpha\beta}-\eta^{\alpha\beta}
in reference
[
211
]
.) One can rewrite equation (
A.25
) in the form (
3
), noting the relationship between
\Pi
and the GW flux in equation (
139
). We can read off the memory contribution to the radiative multipole moment
{\mathcal{}U}_{L}
by comparing equation (
A.25
) to equation (
4
), or read off the shear
\left\langle C^{(1)}_{AB}\right\rangle=\lim_{R\to\infty}(\epsilon R^{-1}\left\langle j_{AB}\right\rangle^{\prime})
.

Finally, we address the dependence on the arbitrary scale
P
, as discussed below equation (
A.20
). Equation (
A.12
) implies that if we change
P
to a new scale
P^{\prime}
, the puncture field
j^{{\mathcal{}P},\mathrm{o}sc.}_{\alpha\beta}
changes by an amount

\Delta j^{{\mathcal{}P},\mathrm{o}sc.}_{\alpha\beta}=-\frac{\ln(P/P^{\prime})}{2r}\left[{\mathcal{}Z}_{uu}(u,\theta^{C})k_{\alpha}k_{\beta}+{\mathcal{}Z}_{AB}(u,\theta^{C})\Omega^{A}_{\alpha}\Omega^{B}_{\beta}\right].

(A.32)

The oscillatory part of the residual field changes by the opposite amount:
\Delta h^{(2){\mathcal{}R},\mathrm{o}sc.}_{\alpha\beta}=-\Delta j^{{\mathcal{}P},\mathrm{o}sc.}_{\alpha\beta}+\mathcal{O}(1/R^{2})
. From the explicit equation (
A.14
), we read off
{\mathcal{}Z}_{AB}=-4M^{(1)}_{\mathrm{B}}\dot{z}^{(1)}_{AB}
. This implies that the change in scale induces the following change in the shear:

\Delta z^{(2){\mathcal{}R}}_{AB}=2M^{(1)}_{\mathrm{B}}\ln(P^{\prime}/P)\dot{z}^{(1)}_{AB},

(A.33)

which is simply proportional to a time derivative of the lower-order shear. If we write the total shear in terms of
lm
modes, as in equation (
56
), we see that the modified waveform is hence given by

\left[\epsilon h^{(1)}_{lm}(\Omega)+\epsilon^{2}h^{(2)\mathcal{}R}_{lm}(\Omega)+\epsilon^{2}\Delta h^{(2)\mathcal{}R}_{lm}(\Omega)+\mathcal{O}(3)\right]e^{-im\phi_{p}}\\
=\left[\epsilon h^{(1)}_{lm}(\Omega)+\epsilon^{2}h^{(2)\mathcal{}R}_{lm}(\Omega)-2im\epsilon^{2}\Omega M^{(1)}_{\mathrm{B}}\ln(P^{\prime}/P)h^{(1)}_{lm}(\Omega)+\mathcal{O}(3)\right]e^{-im\phi_{p}}.

(A.34)

Here we see that we can absorb the change into a redefinition of the phase:

\left[\epsilon h^{(1)}_{lm}(\Omega)+\epsilon^{2}h^{(2)\mathcal{}R}_{lm}(\Omega)+\epsilon^{2}\Delta h^{(2)\mathcal{}R}_{lm}(\Omega)+\mathcal{O}(3)\right]e^{-im\phi_{p}}\\
=\left[\epsilon h^{(1)}_{lm}(\Omega)+\epsilon^{2}h^{(2)\mathcal{}R}_{lm}(\Omega)+\mathcal{O}(3)\right]e^{-im\psi},

(A.35)

where the new phase is

\psi=\phi_{p}+2\epsilon\,\Omega M^{(1)}_{\mathrm{B}}\ln(P^{\prime}/P).

(A.36)

This is a 2PA shift in the waveform phase. Therefore, since we only control the 1PA phase, we can choose
P
to be any convenient value and safely ignore the waveform’s sensitivity to the choice.

Equation (
A.36
) can be compared to the analogous equation (
24
) in the PN-MPM context. Just as the arbitrary scale
b_{0}
drops out of the complete 4PN waveform
[
120
,
123
]
, we expect the arbitrary scale
P
to drop out of the complete 2PA waveform.

The series expressions above are also available in a digital format in the
PostNewtonianSelfForce

[
181
]
package of the
Black Hole Perturbation Toolkit
.

The comparison between the above series and our numerical results is given in figure
B1
below.
As in the case for
l=2
, presented in figure
3
, we find excellent agreement between numerical and series results.

:

but for the
a=0
and

(left column, from top to bottom) and

and

(right column, from top to bottom).

Finally, we present a comparison of a 1PA waveform with memory to an numerical relativity simulation with
q=1
in figure
B2
.
Although the oscillatory part of the waveform dephases significantly close to the merger (as expected for a 1PA waveform at this mass ratio) the memory contribution compares well to the NR waveform until closer to the merger.

. The SXS waveform used in this figure had the ID SXS:BBH:1132
